Global attractor and robust exponential attractors for some classes of fourth-order nonlinear evolution equations
Beniamin Goldys, Agus L. Soenjaya, Thanh Tran

Abstract
. We study the long-time behaviour of solutions to some classes of fourth-order nonlinear PDEs with non-monotone nonlinearities, which include the Landau–Lifshitz–Baryakhtar (LLBar) equation (with all relevant fields and spin torques) and the convective Cahn–Hilliard/Allen–Cahn (CH-AC) equation with a proliferation term, in dimensions d = 1 , 2 , 3 d=1,2,3 . Firstly, we show the global well-posedness, as well as the existence of global and exponential attractors with finite fractal dimensions for these problems. In the case of the exchange-dominated LLBar equation and the CH-AC equation without convection, an estimate for the rate of convergence of the solution to the corresponding stationary state is given. Finally, we show the existence of a robust family of exponential attractors when d ≤ 2 d\leq 2 . As a corollary, exponential attractor of the LLBar equation is shown to converge to that of the Landau–Lifshitz–Bloch equation in the limit of vanishing exchange damping, while exponential attractor of the convective CH-AC equation is shown to converge to that of the convective Allen–Cahn equation in the limit of vanishing diffusion coefficient.



1.
Introduction

This paper aims to show the existence of global attractor and a family of robust exponential attractors for some classes of fourth-order nonlinear PDEs, which include the vector-valued Landau–Lifshitz–Baryakhtar (LLBar) equation with spin-torques and the scalar-valued convective Cahn–Hilliard/Allen–Cahn (CH-AC) equation with a proliferation term, among others. The result also applies to their limiting cases, namely the Landau–Lifshitz–Bloch (LLB) equation with spin-torques and the convective Allen–Cahn equation. The existence of global and exponential attractors of finite fractal dimension allows a reduction, in some sense, of an infinite-dimensional dynamical system to a finite-dimensional one
[
43
]
.

We now describe the general form of the problem discussed in this paper. Let
\mathscr{O}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
d\in\{1,2,3\}
, be an open bounded domain. Let
\boldsymbol{u}(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
, where
m=1
or
3
, be the unknown functions (which can be scalar- or vector-valued). Here,
\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathscr{O}
is the spatial variable, and
t\in(0,T)
is the temporal variable with
T>0
. The boundary of
\mathscr{O}
is denoted by
\partial\mathscr{O}
, with exterior unit normal vector denoted by
\boldsymbol{n}
. The problem can be written as:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}=\sigma\big(\boldsymbol{H}+\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{u})\big)-\varepsilon\Delta\big(\boldsymbol{H}+\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{u})\big)

(1.1a)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\gamma\boldsymbol{u}\times\big(\boldsymbol{H}+\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{u})\big)+\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{u})+\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{u})

for
(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in(0,T)\times\mathscr{O}
,

(1.1b)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{H}=\Psi(\boldsymbol{u})+\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{u})

for
(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in(0,T)\times\mathscr{O}
,

(1.1c)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}(0,\boldsymbol{x})=\boldsymbol{u}_{0}(\boldsymbol{x})

\displaystyle\qquad\text{for }\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathscr{O},

(1.1d)

\displaystyle\displaystyle{\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{u}}{\partial\boldsymbol{n}}=\boldsymbol{0}},\;\displaystyle{\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{H}}{\partial\boldsymbol{n}}=\boldsymbol{0}}

\displaystyle\qquad\text{for }(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in(0,T)\times\partial\mathscr{O}.

The coefficients
\sigma,\varepsilon
, and
\gamma
are positive constants of physical significance, and in particular
\varepsilon
is called the exchange damping coefficient if
m=3
, or the diffusion coefficient if
m=1
. We set
\gamma=0
if
\boldsymbol{u}
is scalar-valued (
m=1
). The terms
\Psi(\boldsymbol{u}),\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{u}),\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{u}),\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{u})
, and
\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{u})
are nonlinear functions of
\boldsymbol{u}
and possibly its spatial gradient, whose exact forms are detailed in Section
2.2
. While we consider the Neumann boundary condition in the above problem, similar arguments will also work for the Dirichlet or the periodic boundary conditions.

When
\boldsymbol{u}
is vector-valued (
m=3
), problem (
1.1
) is the initial-boundary value problem associated with the Landau–Lifshitz–Baryakhtar (LLBar) equation
[
5
,
6
,
7
,
16
]
, which describes the evolution of the magnetisation vectors
\boldsymbol{u}(t,\boldsymbol{x})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
on any point in a magnetic body
\mathscr{O}
at elevated temperatures. The unknown field
\boldsymbol{H}
is called the effective field. Spin-torque effects due to currents
[
52
,
55
]
and anisotropy of the material
[
36
]
are also taken into account. Formally setting
\varepsilon=0
in (
1.1a
) gives the Landau–Lifshitz–Bloch (LLB) equation
[
24
,
25
,
33
]
with spin-torques
[
3
]
, which is a system of quasilinear second-order PDEs. In physical applications, often the limit
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
is taken in the LLBar equation when certain long-range interactions are negligible
[
15
,
52
]
.

When
\boldsymbol{u}
is scalar-valued (
m=1
), we always set
\gamma=0
and
\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{u})=\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{u})=0
. In this case, problem (
1.1
) is the initial-boundary value problem associated with the convective Cahn–Hilliard/Allen–Cahn (CH-AC) equation which models multiple microscopic mechanisms involving diffusion, reaction, transport, and adsorption in cluster interface evolution
[
2
,
28
,
29
,
30
]
. The unknown
\boldsymbol{u}
is often called the order parameter and
\boldsymbol{H}
is the potential.
We further remark that the case
\sigma=0
and
\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{u})=0
gives the convective Cahn–Hilliard (CH) equation
[
18
,
27
]
, while the case
\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{u})=0
gives the Cahn–Hilliard equation with a mass source term
[
23
,
32
,
37
,
39
]
. The term
\mathcal{S}(u)
represents a proliferation term
[
13
,
23
,
38
]
, which is relevant in various biological applications. The problem (
1.1
) also describes a generalised diffusion model for growth and dispersal in a population
[
12
]
. Formally setting
\varepsilon=0
gives a second-order PDE known as the convective Allen–Cahn (AC) equation
[
45
,
46
]
or a reaction-diffusion-convection model with Allee effect in mathematical biology
[
53
]
. Thus, it is also of interest to examine the behaviour of (
1.1
) as
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
if
\boldsymbol{u}
is scalar-valued.

Some mathematical results which are relevant to the present paper will be reviewed here and in the following paragraph. For problem (
1.1
) with
m=3
and
\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{u})=\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{u})=\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{u})=\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{u})=\boldsymbol{0}
, i.e. the LLBar equation, the global existence and uniqueness of strong solution for any finite
T>0
are shown in
[
48
]
(also in
[
26
]
for the stochastic case). Some numerical schemes to approximate the solution are proposed in
[
47
,
49
]
. In the case of the exchange-dominated LLB equation (
\varepsilon=0
), the existence of weak solution is obtained in
[
33
]
, while the existence and uniqueness of strong solution are shown in
[
34
]
. The LLB equation with spin torques is considered in
[
3
]
, where the existence and uniqueness of weak solution for
d\leq 2
were shown under certain assumptions. However, to the best of our knowledge, the analysis of the LLBar or the LLB equations with full effective fields and spin-torques are not available yet in the literature. Asymptotic behaviour of the solutions to these equations in terms of finite-dimensional attractors has not been discussed before either.

For the problem (
1.1
) with
m=1
and
\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{u})=\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{u})=0
, i.e. the CH-AC equation, the existence and uniqueness of weak and strong solutions are shown in
[
29
,
35
]
, while the existence of global attractor in 2D can be established by similar argument as in
[
30
]
.
The existence and uniqueness of solution to the convective CH equation with periodic boundary conditions are shown in
[
18
]
(also in
[
58
]
for the case of unbounded domains), while the existence of global attractor is obtained in
[
18
,
59
]
. We also mention several other papers
[
23
,
32
,
38
,
39
]
, which study the Cahn–Hilliard equation with a polynomial source term. While many results are available in the literature for the scalar-valued CH or CH-AC equations, none of them are sufficiently general to cover the nonlinearities present in problem (
1.1
), especially for
d=3
. Moreover, the limiting case
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
(vanishing diffusion coefficient) has also not been studied.

This paper aims to unify and further develop the analysis of (
1.1
) by deriving the following results:

(i)

global existence and uniqueness of weak and strong solution to (
1.1
) on
(0,T)\times\mathscr{O}
, for any
T>0
(Theorem
3.11
),

(ii)

existence of a (compact) global attractor for (
1.1
) with finite fractal dimension (Theorem
4.10
, Theorem
5.2
),

(iii)

convergence of the solution of the LLBar equation to the corresponding stationary state, with an estimate on the rate of convergence, in the case of exchange-dominated field (Theorem
4.13
),

(iv)

existence of an exponential attractor for (
1.1
) and its characterisation (Theorem
4.16
),

(v)

existence of a robust (in
\varepsilon
) family of exponential attractors for (
1.1
) when
d\leq 2
(Theorem
5.10
).

Existence of a solution to the problem (
1.1
) is obtained by means of the Faedo–Galerkin method. Owing to the nature of nonlinearities present in the problem (which are non-monotone), a detailed analysis is done to derive uniform a priori estimates on the approximate solutions in suitable function spaces, which extend the solution globally in time. The existence of global and exponential attractors is deduced by showing various dissipative and smoothing estimates. To obtain a robust family of exponential attractors, more careful analysis is needed to ensure these estimates are uniform in the parameter
\varepsilon
. We reiterate that while the existence of global solution to the LLBar equation has been shown in
[
48
]
, the model considered there only include the exchange field in
\boldsymbol{H}
and does not consider any convective terms. Most a priori estimates, especially the smoothing estimates and the uniform estimates independent of
\varepsilon
developed in this paper, are new.

As a corollary of our analysis, we deduce the existence of the global attractor and an exponential attractor with finite fractal dimensions for the LLB equations (taking into account all relevant effective fields and spin torques) when
d\leq 2
. In this case, we show that exponential attractor of the LLBar equation converges (in the sense of the symmetric Hausdorff distance) at a given rate to that of the LLB equation as
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
. Similar results are also obtained for the convective Cahn–Hilliard/Allen–Cahn equation, for which the convergence to the convective Allen–Cahn equation is shown in the limit of vanishing diffusion coefficient.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Notations

We begin by defining some notations used in this paper. For
m=1
or
m=3
, the function space
\mathbb{L}^{p}:=\mathbb{L}^{p}(\mathscr{O};\mathbb{R}^{m})
denotes the usual space of
p
-th integrable functions taking values in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
\mathbb{W}^{k,p}:=\mathbb{W}^{k,p}(\mathscr{O};\mathbb{R}^{m})
denotes the usual Sobolev space of
functions on
\mathscr{O}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
taking values in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
. We
write
\mathbb{H}^{k}:=\mathbb{W}^{k,2}
. Here,
\mathscr{O}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
for
d=1,2,3

is an open domain with
C^{\infty}
-smooth boundary. The Laplacian operator acting on
\mathbb{R}^{m}
-valued functions is denoted by
\Delta
.

If
X
is a Banach space, the spaces
L^{p}(0,T;X)
and
W^{k,p}(0,T;X)
denote respectively the usual Lebesgue and Sobolev spaces of functions on
(0,T)
taking values in
X
. The space
C([0,T];X)
denotes the space of continuous functions on
[0,T]
taking values in
X
. Throughout this paper, we denote the scalar product in a Hilbert space
H
by
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{H}
and its corresponding norm by
\|\cdot\|_{H}
. We will not distinguish between the scalar product of
\mathbb{L}^{2}
vector-valued functions taking values in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
and the scalar product of
\mathbb{L}^{2}
matrix-valued functions taking values in
\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
, and still denote them by
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}
.

Throughout this paper, the constant
C
in the estimate always denotes a
generic constant which depends only on the coefficients of (
1.1
),
\mathscr{O}
, and
\nu_{\infty}
(to be defined in (
2.6
)), but is
independent
of
t
. If
the dependence of
C
on some variable, e.g.
T
, is highlighted, we will write

C(T)
. The constants denoted by
C
might take different values at different occurrences, unless otherwise specified.

2.2.
Formulation of the problem and assumptions

In this section, we provide further details on the formulation of problem (
1.1
). Recall that
m=1
or
3
.
The meaning of each term in (
1.1
) is as follows:

(i)

\boldsymbol{u}(t):\mathscr{O}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
is the magnetic spin field if
m=3
, or the order parameter if
m=1
.

(ii)

\boldsymbol{H}(t):\mathscr{O}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
is the effective magnetic field if
m=3
, or the potential if
m=1
.

(iii)

\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{u})
is the convective term defined by

(2.1)

\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{u}):=\beta_{1}(\boldsymbol{\nu}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{u}+\beta_{2}\boldsymbol{u}\times(\boldsymbol{\nu}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{u}+\chi\nabla\cdot(\boldsymbol{u}\otimes\boldsymbol{u}),

where
\boldsymbol{\nu}:\mathscr{O}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is the given current density
[
18
,
27
,
51
]
independent of
t
.

(iv)

\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{u})
consist of other lower-order source term (spin-orbit torque or proliferation term) which grow at most quadratically in
\boldsymbol{u}
, whose properties are detailed in Section
2.2
.

(v)

\Psi(\boldsymbol{u})
is the sum of the exchange field and the Ginzburg–Landau (phase transition) field defined by

(2.2)

\Psi(\boldsymbol{u}):=\Delta\boldsymbol{u}+\kappa_{1}\boldsymbol{u}-\kappa_{2}|\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}\boldsymbol{u},

where
\kappa_{2}>0
.

Two of the terms in (
1.1
) are only relevant when
m=3
, namely:

(i)

\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{u})
is the anisotropy field with cubic nonlinearities given by

(2.3)

\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{u})=\lambda_{1}(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{u})\boldsymbol{e}-\lambda_{2}(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{u})^{3}\boldsymbol{e},

where
\lambda_{2}\geq 0
and
\boldsymbol{e}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
is a given unit vector.

(ii)

The continuous operator
\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}:\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathscr{O})\to\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
defining the demagnetising field satisfies the static Maxwell-Ampère equations on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
:

(2.4)

\begin{cases}\mathrm{curl}\,\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{u})=\boldsymbol{0}&\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{3},\\
\mathrm{div}\,(\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{u})+\boldsymbol{u}\mathds{1}_{\mathscr{O}})=0&\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{3},\end{cases}

where
\boldsymbol{u}\mathds{1}_{\mathscr{O}}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
is an extension of
\boldsymbol{u}
by zero outside of
\mathscr{O}
, namely

\boldsymbol{u}\mathds{1}_{\mathscr{O}}(\boldsymbol{x}):=\begin{cases}\boldsymbol{u}(\boldsymbol{x}),\quad&\text{if }\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathscr{O},\\
\boldsymbol{0},\quad&\text{if }\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\mathscr{O}.\end{cases}

We remark that a more general anisotropy field such as the cubic anisotropy field or the uniaxial anisotropy field with spatially-dependent parameter, as well as other zero order contributions to the field (such as a spatially-varying applied field) can be considered without difficulties, but are omitted for simplicity.

The constants
\beta_{1}
,
\beta_{2}
,
\chi
,
\kappa_{1}
, and
\lambda_{1}
may be positive or negative, but without loss of generality they will be taken as positive throughout the paper. More precisely, according to the Ginzburg–Landau theory, the constant
\kappa_{1}
is positive for temperatures above the Curie temperature and negative below it.

Assumptions made in this paper are stated in the following:

(1)

The constant
\varepsilon
is generally taken to be small (say
\varepsilon\leq 1
), such that
\sigma-(\kappa_{1}+\lambda_{1})\varepsilon>0
for physical reasons and for simplicity of argument. If this is not the case, then the interpolation inequality can be used as in (
3.12
).

(2)

The constant
\chi
in (
2.1
) is assumed to be sufficiently small, say of order
\varepsilon
, or such that

(2.5)

2\chi^{2}<\kappa_{2}\sigma^{2}.

The current density
\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\mathbb{H}^{2}(\mathscr{O};\mathbb{R}^{d})
satisfies

(2.6)

\left\|{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}(\mathscr{O};\mathbb{R}^{d}))}^{2}\leq\nu_{\infty},

for some positive constant
\nu_{\infty}
.

(3)

The constant
\lambda_{1}
in (
2.3
) can be positive or negative, while
\lambda_{2}\geq 0
. More generally, the argument presented here will still hold when
\lambda_{2}<0
such that
\lambda_{2}+\kappa_{2}>0
. Without loss of generality, we assume
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}>0
. In Section
5.2
, we assume
\lambda_{2}=0
.

(4)

The source term
\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{u})
satisfies the following assumptions:

(2.7)

\displaystyle\left|{\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{u})}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C|\boldsymbol{u}|(1+\left|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right|),

(2.8)

\displaystyle\left\|{\nabla\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{u})}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}\right)\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}},

(2.9)

\displaystyle\left\|{\Delta\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{u})}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\right)\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}.

Furthermore, the map
\boldsymbol{u}\mapsto\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{u})
satisfies certain local Lipschitz conditions:

(2.10)

\displaystyle\left\|{\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{v})-\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{w})}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}},\quad\forall\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{w}\in\mathbb{L}^{\infty},

where
C
is a constant depending only on
|\mathscr{O}|
. An example of such map is
\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{u})=\boldsymbol{u}+(\boldsymbol{a}\cdot\boldsymbol{u})\boldsymbol{u}
, where
\boldsymbol{a}
is a given vector in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

The weak formulation of (
1.1
) used in this paper is stated below.

Definition 2.1
.

Given
T>0
and initial data
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in\mathbb{H}^{1}
, a function
\boldsymbol{u}\in C([0,T];\mathbb{H}^{1})\cap L^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{H}^{3})
is a weak solution to problem
(
1.1
) if
\boldsymbol{u}(0)=\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
, and for any
\boldsymbol{\chi}\in\mathbb{H}^{1}
and
t\in(0,T)
,

\displaystyle\left\langle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}(t),\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\sigma\left\langle\boldsymbol{H}(t)+\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{u}(t)),\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\varepsilon\left\langle\nabla\boldsymbol{H}(t),\nabla\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}-\varepsilon\left\langle\Delta\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{u}(t)),\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad-\gamma\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}(t)\times\big(\boldsymbol{H}(t)+\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{u}(t))\big),\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\left\langle\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{u}(t)),\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\left\langle\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{u}(t)),\boldsymbol{\chi}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}},

where

(2.11)

\boldsymbol{H}(t)=\Psi(\boldsymbol{u}(t))+\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{u}(t))\quad\text{in}\quad\mathbb{L}^{2}.

A weak solution
\boldsymbol{u}
is called a strong solution if

\boldsymbol{u}\in C([0,T];\mathbb{H}^{2})\cap L^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{H}^{4}).

In this case,
\boldsymbol{u}
satisfies (
1.1
) almost everywhere in
(0,T)\times\mathscr{O}
.

2.3.
Auxiliary results

In this section, we collect some estimates and identities that will be needed for our analysis.
For any vector-valued functions
\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{w}:\mathscr{O}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
, we have

(2.12)

\displaystyle\nabla(|\boldsymbol{v}|^{2}\boldsymbol{w})

\displaystyle=2\boldsymbol{w}\ (\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{v})+|\boldsymbol{v}|^{2}\nabla\boldsymbol{w},

(2.13)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\big(|\boldsymbol{v}|^{2}\boldsymbol{v}\big)}{\partial\boldsymbol{n}}

\displaystyle=2\boldsymbol{v}\Big(\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{v}}{\partial\boldsymbol{n}}\Big)+|\boldsymbol{v}|^{2}\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{v}}{\partial\boldsymbol{n}},

(2.14)

\displaystyle\Delta(|\boldsymbol{v}|^{2}\boldsymbol{w})

\displaystyle=2|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}|^{2}\boldsymbol{w}+2(\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\Delta\boldsymbol{v})\boldsymbol{w}+4\nabla\boldsymbol{w}\ (\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{v})^{\top}+|\boldsymbol{v}|^{2}\Delta\boldsymbol{w},

provided that the partial derivatives are well defined. As a consequence of (
2.13
), (
2.11
), (
2.2
), (
2.3
), and (
1.1d
), for a sufficiently regular solution
\boldsymbol{u}
of equation (
1.1
),
\partial(\Delta\boldsymbol{u})/\partial\boldsymbol{n}=\boldsymbol{0}
on
\partial\mathscr{O}
.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
\epsilon>0
. There exists a positive constant
C
(depending only on
\mathscr{O}
) such that the following
inequalities hold:

(i)

for any
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathscr{O})
such that
\Delta\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{L}^{2}(\mathscr{O})
and
\partial\boldsymbol{v}/\partial\boldsymbol{n}=0
on
\partial\mathscr{O}
,

(2.15)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\right),

(2.16)

\displaystyle\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{4\epsilon}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

(ii)

for any
\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{w}\in\mathbb{H}^{s}
, where
s>d/2
,

(2.17)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}\odot\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{s}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{s}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{s}},

(2.18)

\displaystyle\left\|{(\boldsymbol{u}\times\boldsymbol{v})\odot\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{s}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{s}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{s}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{s}}.

Here
\odot
denotes either the dot product or cross product in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
.

Proof.

(
2.15
) and (
2.16
) are shown in
[
48
, Lemma 3.3]
, while (
2.17
) and (
2.18
) follow from
[
8
]
.
∎

We show some estimates for the map
\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}
, given in (
2.3
), which defines the anisotropy field in the following lemma.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}
be as defined in (
2.3
), and let
p,q\in[1,\infty]
be such that
2/p+1/q=1/2
.

(1)

For any
\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{w}\in\mathbb{L}^{2}
,

(2.19)

\displaystyle\left\langle\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{v})-\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{w}),\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{1}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

(2)

For any
\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{w}\in\mathbb{L}^{\max\{p,q\}}
,

(2.20)

\displaystyle\left\|{\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{v})-\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{w})}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}^{4}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}^{4}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{q}}^{2}.

(3)

For any
\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{w}\in\mathbb{W}^{1,p}\cap\mathbb{L}^{\infty}
,

\displaystyle\left\|{\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{v})-\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{w})}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{4}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{4}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}

(2.21)

\displaystyle\quad+C\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}^{2}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}^{2}\right)\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{W}^{1,p}}^{2}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{W}^{1,p}}^{2}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{q}}^{2}.

(4)

For any
\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{w}\in\mathbb{H}^{3}
,

\displaystyle\left\|{\Delta\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{v})-\Delta\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{w})}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+C\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{4}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{4}\right)\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{v}-\Delta\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}

(2.22)

\displaystyle\quad+C\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}\right)\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}^{2}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}^{2}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}.

(5)

For any
\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{w}\in\mathbb{H}^{s}
, where
s\geq 2
,

(2.23)

\displaystyle\left\|{\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{v})}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{s}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{s}}^{6}\right).

Proof.

It follows from the definition of
\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}
that

(2.24)

\displaystyle\left\langle\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{v})-\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{w}),\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}=\lambda_{1}\left\|{\boldsymbol{e}\cdot(\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{w})}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}-\lambda_{2}\left\langle(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{v})^{3}\boldsymbol{e}-(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{w})^{3}\boldsymbol{e},\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}.

Note that

(2.25)

\left\langle(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{v})^{3}\boldsymbol{e}-(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{w})^{3}\boldsymbol{e},\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}=(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{v})^{4}+(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{w})^{4}-(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{v})^{3}(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{w})-(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{w})^{3}(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}).

By Young’s inequality,

|(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{v})^{3}(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{w})|\leq\frac{(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{v})^{4}}{4/3}+\frac{(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{w})^{4}}{4}\quad\text{and}\quad|(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{w})^{3}(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{v})|\leq\frac{(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{w})^{4}}{4/3}+\frac{(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{v})^{4}}{4},

and thus from (
2.25
),

\left\langle(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{v})^{3}\boldsymbol{e}-(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{w})^{3}\boldsymbol{e},\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\geq 0.

This, together with (
2.24
), implies (
2.19
).

Next, using the elementary identity

(2.26)

a^{3}-b^{3}=(a-b)(a^{2}+ab+b^{2}),\quad\forall a,b\in\mathbb{R},

we have

\displaystyle\left\|{\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{v})-\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{w})}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\lambda_{1}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+2\lambda_{2}^{2}\left\|{\big(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot(\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{w})\big)\big((\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{v})^{2}+(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{v})(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{w})+(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{w})^{2}\big)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\lambda_{1}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+4\lambda_{2}^{2}\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}^{4}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}^{4}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{q}}^{2},

which implies (
2.20
).

Similarly, by the product rule for derivatives, Hölder’s and Young’s inequalities,

\displaystyle\left\|{\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{v})-\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{w})}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\lambda_{1}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+2\lambda_{2}^{2}\left\|{\big(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot(\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{w})\big)\big((\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{v})^{2}+(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{v})(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{w})+(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{w})^{2}\big)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\lambda_{1}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+4\lambda_{2}^{2}\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}^{4}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}^{4}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{q}}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+4\lambda_{2}^{2}\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{4}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{4}\right)\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}-\nabla\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+4\lambda_{2}^{2}\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}^{2}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}^{2}\right)\left(\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}^{2}+\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{p}}^{2}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{q}}^{2},

which yields (
3
).

Next, we aim to show (
4
). Note that by the identity (
2.26
), writing
a:=\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}
and
b:=\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}
and
\rho:=a-b
, we have

\displaystyle\Delta(a^{3})-\Delta(b^{3})

\displaystyle=(\Delta\rho)(a^{2}+ab+b^{2})+\rho(2a\Delta a+|\nabla a|^{2}+a\Delta b+b\Delta a+2\nabla a\cdot\nabla b+2b\Delta b+|\nabla b|^{2})

\displaystyle\quad+2\nabla\rho\cdot(2a\nabla a+a\nabla b+b\nabla a+2b\nabla b).

Therefore, by Hölder’s inequality,

\displaystyle\left\|{\Delta(a^{3})-\Delta(b^{3})}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\left\|{\Delta\rho}\right\|_{L^{6}}^{2}\left(\left\|{a}\right\|_{L^{6}}^{4}+\left\|{b}\right\|_{L^{6}}^{4}\right)+2\left\|{\rho}\right\|_{L^{6}}^{2}\left(\left\|{a}\right\|_{L^{6}}^{2}+\left\|{b}\right\|_{L^{6}}^{2}\right)\left(\left\|{\Delta a}\right\|_{L^{6}}^{2}+\left\|{\Delta b}\right\|_{L^{6}}^{2}\right)

(2.27)

\displaystyle\quad+2\left\|{\rho}\right\|_{L^{6}}^{2}\left(\left\|{\nabla a}\right\|_{L^{6}}^{4}+\left\|{\nabla b}\right\|_{L^{6}}^{4}\right)+8\left\|{\nabla\rho}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\left(\left\|{a}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}+\left\|{b}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\right)\left(\left\|{\nabla a}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}+\left\|{\nabla b}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\right).

Note that by the Gagliardo–Nirenberg and interpolation inequalities, we have for any function
f\in H^{3}(\mathscr{O})
,

\displaystyle\left\|{f}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{f}\right\|_{H^{1}}\left\|{f}\right\|_{H^{2}}\leq C\left\|{f}\right\|_{H^{1}}^{3/2}\left\|{f}\right\|_{H^{3}}^{1/2},

\displaystyle\left\|{\nabla f}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\nabla f}\right\|_{H^{1}}\left\|{\nabla f}\right\|_{H^{2}}\leq C\left\|{f}\right\|_{H^{1}}^{1/2}\left\|{f}\right\|_{H^{3}}^{3/2},

\displaystyle\left\|{\nabla f}\right\|_{L^{6}}^{4}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{f}\right\|_{H^{2}}^{4}\leq C\left\|{f}\right\|_{H^{1}}^{2}\left\|{f}\right\|_{H^{3}}^{2}.

Using these inequalities in (
2.3
) and applying the Sobolev embedding
H^{1}\hookrightarrow L^{6}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|{\Delta(a^{3})-\Delta(b^{3})}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\left\|{a}\right\|_{H^{1}}^{4}+\left\|{b}\right\|_{H^{1}}^{4}\right)\left\|{\Delta\rho}\right\|_{H^{1}}^{2}+C\left(\left\|{a}\right\|_{H^{1}}^{2}+\left\|{b}\right\|_{H^{1}}^{2}\right)\left(\left\|{a}\right\|_{H^{3}}^{2}+\left\|{b}\right\|_{H^{3}}^{2}\right)\left\|{\rho}\right\|_{H^{1}}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+C\left(\left\|{a}\right\|_{H^{1}}^{3/2}\left\|{a}\right\|_{H^{3}}^{1/2}+\left\|{b}\right\|_{H^{1}}^{3/2}\left\|{b}\right\|_{H^{3}}^{1/2}\right)\left(\left\|{a}\right\|_{H^{1}}^{1/2}\left\|{a}\right\|_{H^{3}}^{3/2}+\left\|{b}\right\|_{H^{1}}^{1/2}\left\|{b}\right\|_{H^{3}}^{3/2}\right)\left\|{\nabla\rho}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\left\|{a}\right\|_{H^{1}}^{4}+\left\|{b}\right\|_{H^{1}}^{4}\right)\left\|{\Delta\rho}\right\|_{H^{1}}^{2}+C\left(\left\|{a}\right\|_{H^{1}}^{2}+\left\|{b}\right\|_{H^{1}}^{2}\right)\left(\left\|{a}\right\|_{H^{3}}^{2}+\left\|{b}\right\|_{H^{3}}^{2}\right)\left\|{\rho}\right\|_{H^{1}}^{2}

(2.28)

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{4}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{4}\right)\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{v}-\Delta\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+C\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}\right)\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}^{2}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}^{2}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}.

where in the last step we used Young’s inequality. Hence, by the triangle inequality,

\displaystyle\left\|{\Delta\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{v})-\Delta\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{w})}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{1}^{2}\left\|{\rho\boldsymbol{e}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\lambda_{2}^{2}\left\|{\left(\Delta(a^{3})-\Delta(b^{3})\right)\boldsymbol{e}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\lambda_{1}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}-\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\lambda_{2}^{2}\left\|{\Delta(a^{3})-\Delta(b^{3})}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2},

and thus the required inequality (
4
) follows from (
2.3
).

Finally, inequality (
2.23
) follows by applying (
2.18
) to the definition of
\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{v})
.
∎

Next, we prove several estimates related to the map
\mathcal{R}
(which defines the spin torque term, see (
2.1
)) in the following lemmas.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\mathbb{H}^{2}(\mathscr{O};\mathbb{R}^{d})
be given, satisfying assumption (
2.6
). Let
\mathcal{R}
be the map defined by (
2.1
).
For any
\epsilon,\sigma>0
, there exists a positive constant
C_{\epsilon}
such that for any
\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{w}\in\mathbb{H}^{1}\cap\mathbb{L}^{\infty}
,

(2.29)

\displaystyle\big|\left\langle\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\big|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\epsilon}\nu_{\infty}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\chi^{2}\sigma^{-1}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{4}+\epsilon\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\sigma}{4}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

(2.30)

\displaystyle\big|\left\langle\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\big|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\epsilon}\nu_{\infty}\left(\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\left\|{|\boldsymbol{v}|\,|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}|}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\right)+\epsilon\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

(2.31)

\displaystyle\big|\left\langle\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{v})-\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{w}),\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\big|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\epsilon}\nu_{\infty}\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{2}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}-\nabla\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

where
\nu_{\infty}
was defined in (
2.6
). Furthermore, for any
\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{w}\in\mathbb{H}^{2k}
, where
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

(2.32)

\displaystyle\Big|\left\langle\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{v})-\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{w}),\Delta^{k}\boldsymbol{v}-\Delta^{k}\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\epsilon}\nu_{\infty}\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\Delta^{k}\boldsymbol{v}-\Delta^{k}\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

Proof.

These can be shown in the same manner as in
[
26
, Lemma 2.3]
.
∎

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\mathbb{H}^{2}(\mathscr{O};\mathbb{R}^{d})
be given, satisfying assumption (
2.6
). There exists a positive constant
C
such that for sufficiently regular
\boldsymbol{v}
,

(2.33)

\displaystyle\left\|{\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{v})}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\nu_{\infty}\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{4}+\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{4}\right),

(2.34)

\displaystyle\left\|{\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{v})}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\nu_{\infty}\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}^{2}.

Proof.

We first show (
2.33
).
By Hölder’s inequality,

\displaystyle\left\|{\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{v})}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}(\mathscr{O};\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{2}+C\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}(\mathscr{O};\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}^{2}

(2.35)

\displaystyle\leq C\nu_{\infty}\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}\right)\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

Next, by Hölder’s and Young’s inequalities, and the Sobolev embedding, we have

\displaystyle\left\|{\nabla\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{v})}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\beta_{1}^{2}\big(\left\|{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}(\mathscr{O};\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}+\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{\nu}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{3}(\mathscr{O};\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}^{2}\big)

\displaystyle\quad+(\beta_{2}+\chi)^{2}\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}(\mathscr{O};\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{4}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}^{2}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{\nu}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}^{2}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}^{2}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}(\mathscr{O};\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}\right)

(2.36)

\displaystyle\leq C\nu_{\infty}\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{4}+\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{4}\right),

Adding (
2.3
) and (
2.3
) gives (
2.33
).

Finally, inequality (
2.34
) follows by applying (
2.18
) to the definition of
\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{v})
.
∎

Further estimates for the map
\mathcal{R}
in the case
d\leq 2
are stated below. These will be needed in Section
5.2
.

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
d\leq 2
and
\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\mathbb{H}^{2}(\mathscr{O};\mathbb{R}^{d})
be given, satisfying assumption (
2.6
). Let
\mathcal{R}
be the map defined by (
2.1
).
For any
\epsilon>0
, there exists a positive constant
C_{\epsilon}
such that for sufficiently regular
\boldsymbol{v}
,

(2.37)

\displaystyle\big|\left\langle\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{v}),\Delta\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\big|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\epsilon}\nu_{\infty}\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{4}\right)\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

(2.38)

\displaystyle\big|\left\langle\nabla\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{v}),\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\big|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\epsilon}\nu_{\infty}\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{4}+\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{4}\right)+\epsilon\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

(2.39)

\displaystyle\big|\left\langle\Delta\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{v}),\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\big|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\epsilon}\nu_{\infty}\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{4}\right)+C_{\epsilon}\nu_{\infty}\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}\right)\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

where
\nu_{\infty}
was defined in (
2.6
). Furthermore, for sufficiently regular
\boldsymbol{v}
and
\boldsymbol{w}
,

\displaystyle\big|\left\langle\nabla\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{v})-\nabla\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{w}),\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{v}-\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\big|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\epsilon}\nu_{\infty}\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}

(2.40)

\displaystyle\quad+\epsilon\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{v}-\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

Proof.

Firstly, by Hölder’s and Young’s inequalities, and the Gagliardo–Nirenberg inequalities (for
d\leq 2
) we obtain

\displaystyle\big|\left\langle\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{v}),\Delta\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\big|

\displaystyle\leq\beta_{1}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}(\mathscr{O};\mathbb{R}^{d})}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\left(\beta_{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}(\mathscr{O};\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\chi\right)\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{1/2}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{3/2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C_{\epsilon}\nu_{\infty}\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{4}\right)\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

showing (
2.37
). Next, (
2.38
) follows from (
2.33
) and Young’s inequality. Similarly, the estimate (
2.39
) can be deduced from (
2.34
) and Young’s inequality. The inequality (
2.6
) can be shown in a similar manner as (
2.32
) and (
2.38
).
This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Next, estimates on
\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{u})
are stated in the following lemma.

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be the map satisfying (
2.7
) and
k\geq 0
. For any
\epsilon>0
, there exists a positive constant
C_{\epsilon}
such that for sufficiently regular
\boldsymbol{v}
and
\boldsymbol{w}
,

(2.41)

\displaystyle\big|\left\langle\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\big|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\epsilon}\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{4}\right)+\epsilon\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

(2.42)

\displaystyle\big|\left\langle\nabla\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{v}),\nabla\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\big|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\epsilon}\left(\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\left\|{|\boldsymbol{v}||\nabla\boldsymbol{v}|}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\right)+\epsilon\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

(2.43)

\displaystyle\big|\left\langle\Delta\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{v}),\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\big|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\epsilon}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{4}+C_{\epsilon}\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{2}\right)\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

(2.44)

\displaystyle\Big|\left\langle\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{v})-\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{w}),\Delta^{k}\boldsymbol{v}-\Delta^{k}\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\epsilon}\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{2}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{2}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\Delta^{k}\boldsymbol{v}-\Delta^{k}\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

(2.45)

\displaystyle\Big|\left\langle\nabla\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{v})-\nabla\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{w}),\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{v}-\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\epsilon}\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+\epsilon\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{v}-\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

Proof.

These inequalities follow from (
2.7
), (
2.8
), (
2.9
), (
2.10
), and Young’s inequality. Inequality (
2.45
) can be shown in a similar manner as (
2.6
).
∎

Some properties of the operator
\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}
defining the demagnetising field are recalled below (see also
[
42
]
).

Theorem 2.8
.

The solution to (
2.4
) can be written as

(2.46)

\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{v})=-\nabla\big(G\ast\text{div}(\boldsymbol{v})\big),

where
G
is the fundamental solution of the Laplace operator and
\ast
denotes the convolution operator.
Furthermore, the following statements hold

(1)

For any
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{H}^{s}(\mathscr{O})
, where
s\geq 0
, we have

(2.47)

\displaystyle\left\|{\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{v})}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}\leq\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{s}(\mathscr{O})}.

(2)

If
\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{W}^{k,p}(\mathscr{O}
) for some
p\in(1,\infty)
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
, then the restriction of
\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{v})
to
\mathscr{O}
belongs to
\mathbb{W}^{k,p}(\mathscr{O})
and satisfies

(2.48)

\displaystyle\left\|{\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{v})}\right\|_{\mathbb{W}^{k,p}(\mathscr{O})}\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{W}^{k,p}(\mathscr{O})},

where the positive constant
C
is independent of
\boldsymbol{v}
.

Proof.

Refer to
[
44
, Section 2.5]
,
[
9
, Lemma 2.3]
and
[
14
, Lemma 3.1]
.
∎

2.4.
Faedo–Galerkin method

The Faedo–Galerkin approximation will be used to establish the existence of solution to (
1.1
).
Let
\{\boldsymbol{e}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
denote an orthonormal basis of
\mathbb{L}^{2}

consisting of eigenfunctions of
-\Delta
such that

\displaystyle-\Delta\boldsymbol{e}_{i}=\mu_{i}\boldsymbol{e}_{i}\ \text{ in $\mathscr{O}$}\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{e}_{i}}{\partial\boldsymbol{n}}=\boldsymbol{0}\ \text{ on }\partial\mathscr{O},\quad\forall i\in\mathbb{N},

where
\mu_{i}
are the eigenvalues of
-\Delta
associated with
\boldsymbol{e}_{i}
.

Let
\mathbb{V}_{n}:=\text{span}\{\boldsymbol{e_{1}},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e_{n}}\}
and
\Pi_{n}:\mathbb{L}^{2}\to\mathbb{V}_{n}
be the orthogonal projection defined by

\displaystyle\left\langle\Pi_{n}\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{\phi}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}=\left\langle\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{\phi}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}},\quad\forall\boldsymbol{\phi}\in\mathbb{V}_{n},\;\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{L}^{2}.

Note that
\Pi_{n}
is self-adjoint and satisfies

\displaystyle\left\|{\Pi_{n}\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}},\quad\forall\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{L}^{2},

\displaystyle\left\|{\nabla\Pi_{n}\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}},\quad\forall\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{H}^{1}.

Also,

\left\langle\Pi_{n}\Delta\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{\phi}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}=\left\langle\Delta\Pi_{n}\boldsymbol{v},\boldsymbol{\phi}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}},\quad\forall\boldsymbol{\phi}\in\mathbb{V}_{n},\;\boldsymbol{v}\in\text{D}(\Delta).

The Faedo–Galerkin method seeks to approximate the solution
to (
1.1
) by
\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)\in\mathbb{V}_{n}
satisfying the equation

(2.49)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}=\sigma\big(\boldsymbol{H}_{n}+\Pi_{n}\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{n})\big)-\varepsilon\Delta\boldsymbol{H}_{n}-\varepsilon\Pi_{n}\Delta\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{n})&&\\
&\qquad\qquad-\gamma\Pi_{n}\big(\boldsymbol{u}_{n}\times(\boldsymbol{H}_{n}+\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{n}))\big)+\Pi_{n}\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{u}_{n})+\Pi_{n}\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{u}_{n})&&\qquad\text{in $(0,T)\times\mathscr{O}$,}\\
&\boldsymbol{H}_{n}=\Pi_{n}\big(\Psi(\boldsymbol{u}_{n})\big)+\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{n})\big)&&\qquad\text{in $(0,T)\times\mathscr{O}$,}\\
&\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(0)=\boldsymbol{u}_{0n}&&\qquad\text{in $\mathscr{O}$,}\end{aligned}\right.

where the maps
\mathcal{R}
,
\mathcal{S}
,
\Psi
,
\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}
, and
\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}
are specified in Section
2.2
, and
\boldsymbol{u}_{0n}:=\Pi_{n}\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in\mathbb{V}_{n}
.

The existence of solutions to the above ordinary differential equation in
\mathbb{V}_{n}
defined on the interval
(0,t_{n})\subseteq(0,T)
is guaranteed by the Cauchy–Lipschitz theorem. In the next section, we will prove several a priori estimates, which are used to ensure the Faedo–Galerkin solutions
(\boldsymbol{u}_{n},\boldsymbol{H}_{n})
can be continued globally to
(0,+\infty)
for any initial data
\boldsymbol{u}_{0n}\in\mathbb{V}_{n}
.

3.
Uniform estimates

In the following, we will derive various estimates on
\boldsymbol{u}_{n}
and
\boldsymbol{H}_{n}
which are uniform in
n
to
show global existence and uniqueness of solution to (
1.1
),
as well as the existence of an absorbing set.
Several types of bounds are proved in this section, namely for
k=0,1,2,3
, we derive:

(1)

bounds for
\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(0,T;\mathbb{H}^{k})}
which depend on
\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{k}}
, and bounds for
\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{L^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{H}^{k+2})}
which depend on
\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{k}}
and
T
,

(2)

for sufficiently large
t_{k}:=t_{k}(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})
, bounds for
\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(t_{k},\infty;\mathbb{H}^{k})}
which are independent of
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
and
t
,

(3)

bounds for
\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(0,\infty;\mathbb{H}^{k})}
which depend on
\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{k-1}}
, but are independent of
\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{k}}
.

Corresponding estimates for
\boldsymbol{H}_{n}
will also be shown. These will be essential in the proof of existence of attractors in the next section.
For ease of presentation, we often omit the dependence of the functions on
t
in the proof of these estimates. For some of the estimates, we highlight the dependence of the constant on
\varepsilon
as this will be used subsequently.

Proposition 3.1
.

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
t\geq 0
, the following bounds hold:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{4}\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)||\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)|}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s

(3.1)

\displaystyle\leq C(1+t)\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

where
C
is independent of
\varepsilon
and
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
, and

(3.2)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq M,

where
M
depends on
\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}
, but is independent of
\varepsilon
and
t
.

Furthermore, there exists
t_{0}
depending on
\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}
such that for all
t\geq t_{0}
,

(3.3)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\int_{t}^{t+1}\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{4}+\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)||\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)|}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}s\leq\rho_{0},

where the constant
\rho_{0}
is independent of
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
,
\varepsilon
, and
t
.

Finally, for any
t\geq 0
and
\delta>0
,

(3.4)

\displaystyle\int_{t}^{t+\delta}\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{4}+\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)||\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)|}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}s\leq\mu_{0},

where the constant
\mu_{0}
depends only on
M
and
\delta
.

Proof.

Taking the inner product of the first equation in (
2.49
) with
\boldsymbol{u}_{n}
and integrating by parts give

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sigma\left\langle\boldsymbol{H}_{n},\boldsymbol{u}_{n}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\sigma\left\langle\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{n}),\boldsymbol{u}_{n}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\varepsilon\left\langle\nabla\boldsymbol{H}_{n},\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}-\varepsilon\left\langle\Delta\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{n}),\boldsymbol{u}_{n}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

(3.5)

\displaystyle\quad+\left\langle\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{u}_{n}),\boldsymbol{u}_{n}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\left\langle\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{u}_{n}),\boldsymbol{u}_{n}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

Taking the inner product of the second equation in (
2.49
)
with
\boldsymbol{u}_{n}
and
\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}
, successively, give

(3.6)

\displaystyle\sigma\left\langle\boldsymbol{H}_{n},\boldsymbol{u}_{n}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle=-\sigma\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\kappa_{1}\sigma\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}-\kappa_{2}\sigma\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{4}+\lambda_{1}\left\|{\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}-\lambda_{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{4}

\displaystyle\varepsilon\left\langle\nabla\boldsymbol{H}_{n},\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle=-\varepsilon\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon\kappa_{1}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}-\varepsilon\kappa_{2}\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}_{n}||\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}|}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}-2\varepsilon\kappa_{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

(3.7)

\displaystyle\quad+\varepsilon\lambda_{1}\left\|{\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}-3\varepsilon\lambda_{2}\left\|{(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{n})(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n})}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

Adding equations (
3
), (
3.6
), and (
3
), we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\sigma\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon\kappa_{2}\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}_{n}||\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}|}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+2\varepsilon\kappa_{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\kappa_{2}\sigma\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{4}

\displaystyle=\sigma\left\langle\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{n}),\boldsymbol{u}_{n}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}-\varepsilon\left\langle\Delta\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{n}),\boldsymbol{u}_{n}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\left\langle\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{u}_{n}),\boldsymbol{u}_{n}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\kappa_{1}\sigma\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon\kappa_{1}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\lambda_{1}\left\|{\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}-\lambda_{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{4}+\varepsilon\lambda_{1}\left\|{\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}-3\varepsilon\lambda_{2}\left\|{(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{n})(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n})}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\left\langle\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{u}_{n}),\boldsymbol{u}_{n}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

(3.8)

\displaystyle=I_{1}+I_{2}+\cdots+I_{10}.

For the first and the second term, we apply Hölder’s inequality and (
2.47
) to obtain

(3.9)

\displaystyle\left|{I_{1}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sigma\left\|{\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{n})}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

(3.10)

\displaystyle\left|{I_{2}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon\left\|{\Delta\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{n})}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\leq C\varepsilon\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

For the term
I_{3}
, by (
2.29
),

(3.11)

\displaystyle\left|{I_{3}}\right|\leq C\nu_{\infty}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\chi^{2}\sigma^{-1}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{4}+\frac{\sigma}{4}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

For the terms
I_{5}
and
I_{8}
, by Young’s inequality and interpolation inequality (
2.16
), we have

(3.12)

\displaystyle\left|{I_{5}}\right|+\left|{I_{8}}\right|\leq C\varepsilon\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

For the last term, by (
2.7
) we have

(3.13)

\displaystyle\left|{I_{10}}\right|\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+C\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{3}}^{3}+\frac{\sigma}{4}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

Substituting (
3.9
), (
3.10
), (
3.11
), and (
3.12
) into (
3
) (noting assumption (
2.5
) and that
\boldsymbol{e}
is a unit vector), then rearranging the terms give

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\sigma\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon\kappa_{2}\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}_{n}||\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}|}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle+\kappa_{1}\sigma\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\kappa_{2}\sigma\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{4}\leq C\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{3}}^{3}\right)

which can be rearranged into

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\sigma\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon\kappa_{2}\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}_{n}||\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}|}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle+\kappa_{1}\sigma\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\kappa_{2}\sigma\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{4}

(3.14)

\displaystyle\leq C\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{3}}^{3}\right)-\frac{1}{2}\kappa_{2}\sigma\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{4},

where
C
is independent of
\varepsilon
. Now, note that for any
\alpha,\beta>0
, we have

\displaystyle\alpha\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{3}}^{3}-\beta\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{4}=\int_{\mathscr{O}}\alpha|\boldsymbol{u}_{n}|^{3}-\beta|\boldsymbol{u}_{n}|^{4}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\frac{\alpha^{4}|\mathscr{O}|}{\beta^{3}}.

Using this inequality with
\alpha=C
and
\beta=\kappa_{2}\sigma/2
, we obtain from (
3
),

(3.15)

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+2\sigma\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon\kappa_{2}\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}_{n}||\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}|}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\kappa_{1}\sigma\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\kappa_{2}\sigma\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{4}\leq C.

The Gronwall inequality then yields

\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq e^{-\kappa_{1}\sigma t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+C(\kappa_{1}\sigma)^{-1},

which implies the existence of positive constants
M
and
\rho_{0}
such that

(3.16)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq M\quad\text{and}\quad\limsup_{t\to\infty}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{4}\rho_{0}.

Here,
M
depends on
\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}
but is independent of
\varepsilon
, while
\rho_{0}
is independent of
\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}
. Thus, the inequality (
3.2
) is shown. Integrating (
3.15
) over
(0,t)
and rearranging the terms then yield (
3.1
).

To prove (
3.3
), we note that the second inequality in (
3.16
) also implies

(3.17)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{2}\rho_{0},\quad\forall t\geq t_{0},

for some sufficiently large
t_{0}
(depending on
\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}
).
Integrating (
3.15
) over

(t,t+1)
, rearranging the terms, and using (
3.17
). we obtain the second
inequality in (
3.3
). By the same argument, but using (
3.2
) and integrating over
(t,t+\delta)
instead, we obtain (
3.4
). This completes the proof of the proposition.
∎

The following proposition establishes a parabolic smoothing estimate, which will be used later to deduce the existence of an exponential attractor and obtain an estimate for the dimension of the attractor.

Proposition 3.2
.

For all
t>0
,

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq M_{0}\varepsilon^{-1}(1+t+t^{-1}),

where
M_{0}
depends only on the coefficients of (
1.1
),
|\mathscr{O}|
,
\nu_{\infty}
, and
\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}
(but is independent of
\varepsilon
).

Proof.

Taking the inner product of the first equation in (
2.49
) with
\boldsymbol{H}_{n}
and the second equation with
\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left\langle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n},\boldsymbol{H}_{n}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\sigma\left\|{\boldsymbol{H}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{H}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\sigma\left\langle\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{n}),\boldsymbol{H}_{n}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\varepsilon\left\langle\Delta\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{n}),\boldsymbol{H}_{n}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

(3.18)

\displaystyle\quad-\gamma\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}_{n}\times\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{n}),\boldsymbol{H}_{n}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\left\langle\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{u}_{n}),\boldsymbol{H}_{n}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\left\langle\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{u}_{n}),\boldsymbol{H}_{n}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}},

\displaystyle\left\langle\boldsymbol{H}_{n},\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\kappa_{1}}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}-\frac{\kappa_{2}}{4}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{4}

(3.19)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\lambda_{1}}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}-\frac{\lambda_{2}}{4}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{4}.

For the third and the fourth term on the right-hand side of (
3
), by Young’s inequality and (
2.47
),

\displaystyle\sigma\,\Big|\left\langle\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{n}),\boldsymbol{H}_{n}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\Big|+\varepsilon\,\Big|\left\langle\Delta\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{n}),\boldsymbol{H}_{n}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\Big|\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+C\varepsilon\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\sigma}{8}\left\|{\boldsymbol{H}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

For the fifth term on the right-hand side of (
3
), we use (
2.48
) and Young’s inequality to obtain

\gamma\,\Big|\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}_{n}\times\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{n}),\boldsymbol{H}_{n}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\Big|\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{4}+\frac{\sigma}{8}\left\|{\boldsymbol{H}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

For the last two terms in (
3
), we apply (
2.30
) and (
2.41
) respectively. Altogether, from (
3
) and (
3
) we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\kappa_{2}}{4}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}_{n}|^{2}-\kappa_{1}/\kappa_{2}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\lambda_{2}}{4}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left\|{(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{n})^{2}-\lambda_{1}/\lambda_{2}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\sigma\left\|{\boldsymbol{H}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{H}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

(3.20)

\displaystyle\quad\leq C\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{4}\right)+C\varepsilon\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+C\nu_{\infty}\left(\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}_{n}|\,|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}|}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\right)+\frac{\sigma}{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{H}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

which implies

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq C\nu_{\infty}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+C\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{4}+\varepsilon\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\nu_{\infty}\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}_{n}|\,|\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}|}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\right).

On the other hand, due to (
3.4
), we have

\int_{t}^{t+1}\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{4}+\varepsilon\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)||\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)|}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}s\leq\mu_{0}.

Therefore, by using Corollary
A.2
and noting (
3.1
), we obtain, for any
t\geq 0
,

\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq C\varepsilon^{-1}t+C\varepsilon^{-1}(1+t^{-1}),

which implies the required result.
∎

Proposition 3.3
.

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
t\geq 0
,

(3.21)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon^{-1}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2},

(3.22)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{H}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{H}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s+\varepsilon^{2}\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon^{-1}(1+t^{3})\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2},

where
C
is a constant which is independent of
\varepsilon
,
t
, and
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
.

Moreover, there exists
t_{1}
depending on
\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}
such that for all
t\geq t_{1}
,

(3.23)

\displaystyle\varepsilon\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+\int_{t}^{t+1}\left(\varepsilon\left\|{\boldsymbol{H}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{H}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon^{3}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}s\leq\rho_{1},

where
\rho_{1}
is independent of
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
,
\varepsilon
, and
t
.

Finally, let
\delta>0
be arbitrary. Then for all
t\geq\delta
,

(3.24)

\varepsilon\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+\int_{t}^{t+\delta}\left(\varepsilon\left\|{\boldsymbol{H}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{H}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon^{3}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}s\leq\mu_{1},

where
\mu_{1}
depends on
\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}
, but is independent of
\varepsilon
and
t
.

Proof.

Integrating (
3
) with respect to
t
and rearranging (noting (
3.1
)), we obtain

(3.25)

\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+\sigma\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{H}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{H}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s\leq C\varepsilon^{-1}(1+t)\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}.

Note that the bound for
\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}
still depends on
t
.
Taking the inner product of the second equation in (
2.49
) with
\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}
and integrating by parts as necessary give

\displaystyle\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\kappa_{1}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\left\langle\nabla\boldsymbol{H}_{n},\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\kappa_{2}\left\langle\nabla(|\boldsymbol{u}_{n}|^{2}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}),\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad-\lambda_{1}\left\langle\boldsymbol{e}(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}),\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+3\lambda_{2}\left\langle\boldsymbol{e}(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{n})^{2}\boldsymbol{e}(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}),\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\kappa_{1}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{H}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\kappa_{2}\left\|{\nabla(|\boldsymbol{u}_{n}|^{2}\boldsymbol{u}_{n})}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+\lambda_{1}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+3\lambda_{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}^{2}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}+C\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{H}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+C\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}^{4}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

(3.26)

\displaystyle\leq C\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{4}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}+C\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{H}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

where in the penultimate step we used Young’s inequality and the Sobolev embedding
\mathbb{H}^{1}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{L}^{6}
. Rearranging the terms, integrating both sides over
(0,t)
, then applying (
3.25
) and (
3.1
), we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s\leq C\varepsilon^{-3}(1+t^{3})\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}.

This, together with (
3.25
) shows (
3.22
).

Next, from (
3
) and noting (
3.4
), we have by the uniform Gronwall inequality that,

(3.27)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)|^{2}-\kappa_{1}/\kappa_{2}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq C\varepsilon^{-1},\quad\forall t\geq\delta,

where
C
is independent of
\varepsilon
.
We now take
\delta=1
. For
t\in(0,1)
, (
3.25
) gives

\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}\leq C\varepsilon^{-1}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2},

where
C
is independent of
t
and
\varepsilon
. This bound, together with (
3.27
), yields (
3.21
).

Noting (
3.3
), we apply the uniform Gronwall inequality to (
3
) to obtain

(3.28)

\displaystyle\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\kappa_{2}}{2}\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}_{n}|^{2}-\kappa_{1}/\kappa_{2}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq C\varepsilon^{-1},\quad\forall t\geq t_{0}+1,

where
C
is independent of
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
,
\varepsilon
, and
t
.
Inserting this into (
3
) and integrating over
(t,t+1)
give

(3.29)

\displaystyle\int_{t}^{t+1}\sigma\left\|{\boldsymbol{H}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{H}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s\leq C\varepsilon^{-1},\quad\forall t\geq t_{0}+1.

Now, integrating (
3
) over
(t,t+1)
and rearranging the terms (and noting (
3.28
)), we obtain

(3.30)

\displaystyle\int_{t}^{t+1}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s\leq C\varepsilon^{-3},\quad\forall t\geq t_{0}+1,

where
C
is independent of
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
,
\varepsilon
, and
t
. Altogether, (
3.28
), (
3.29
), and (
3.30
) yield (
3.23
).

By similar argument, but integrating (
3
) over
(t,t+\delta)
instead and applying (
3.27
), we obtain (
3.24
).
This completes the proof of the proposition.
∎

Proposition 3.4
.

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
t\geq 0
,

(3.31)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{H}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon^{-4}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2},

(3.32)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon^{-3}(1+t^{3})\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2},

where
C
is a constant which is independent of
\varepsilon
,
t
, and
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
.

Moreover, there exists
t_{2}
depending on
\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}
such that for all
t\geq t_{2}
,

(3.33)

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{4}\left\|{\boldsymbol{H}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\int_{t}^{t+1}\varepsilon^{3}\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s\leq\rho_{2}.

Here,
\rho_{2}
is independent of
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
,
\varepsilon
, and
t
.

Finally, let
\delta>0
be arbitrary. Then for all
t\geq\delta
,

(3.34)

\varepsilon^{4}\left\|{\boldsymbol{H}_{n}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\int_{t}^{t+\delta}\varepsilon^{3}\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s\leq\mu_{2},

where
\mu_{2}
depends on
\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}
, but is independent of
\varepsilon
and
t
.

Proof.

Taking the inner product of the first equation in (
2.49
) with
\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}
yields

\displaystyle\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sigma\left\langle\boldsymbol{H}_{n},\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\varepsilon\left\langle\nabla\boldsymbol{H}_{n},\nabla\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\sigma\left\langle\Pi_{n}\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{n}),\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad-\varepsilon\left\langle\Delta\Pi_{n}\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{n}),\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}-\gamma\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}_{n}\times\boldsymbol{H}_{n},\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}-\gamma\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}_{n}\times\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{n}),\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

(3.35)

\displaystyle\quad+\left\langle\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{u}_{n}),\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\left\langle\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{u}_{n}),\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}.

Differentiating the second equation in (
2.49
) with respect to
t
, then taking inner product with
\varepsilon\boldsymbol{H}_{n}
yields

\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{H}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=-\varepsilon\left\langle\nabla\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n},\nabla\boldsymbol{H}_{n}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\kappa_{1}\varepsilon\left\langle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n},\boldsymbol{H}_{n}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}-\kappa_{2}\varepsilon\left\langle\partial_{t}(|\boldsymbol{u}_{n}|^{2}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}),\boldsymbol{H}_{n}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+\lambda_{1}\varepsilon\left\langle(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n})\boldsymbol{e},\boldsymbol{H}_{n}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}-3\lambda_{2}\varepsilon\left\langle(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{u}_{n})^{2}\boldsymbol{e}(\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}),\boldsymbol{H}_{n}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

Adding this to (
3
), then applying Hölder’s and Young’s inequalities give

\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{H}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\left\|{\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=(\sigma+\kappa_{1}\varepsilon)\left\langle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n},\boldsymbol{H}_{n}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\sigma\left\langle\Pi_{n}\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{n}),\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}-\varepsilon\left\langle\Delta\Pi_{n}\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{u}_{n}),\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}_{n}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}
