t\sigma\Delta\boldsymbol{w}
, we have

\displaystyle t\sigma\left\langle\Delta\boldsymbol{B},\Delta\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle=-t\sigma\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+t\kappa_{1}\sigma\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}-t\kappa_{2}\sigma\left\langle\Delta(|\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}\boldsymbol{w}),\Delta\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

(4.18)

\displaystyle\quad+t\kappa_{2}\sigma\left\langle\Delta\big(((\boldsymbol{u}+\boldsymbol{v})\cdot\boldsymbol{w})\boldsymbol{v}\big),\Delta\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+t\sigma\left\langle\Delta\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{u})-\Delta\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{v}),\Delta\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}.

Similarly, applying the operator
\Delta
to (
4.4
) then taking the inner product of the result with
-t\varepsilon\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{w}
, we obtain

\displaystyle-t\varepsilon\left\langle\Delta\boldsymbol{B},\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle=-t\varepsilon\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+t\kappa_{1}\varepsilon\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+t\kappa_{2}\varepsilon\left\langle\Delta\big(|\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}\boldsymbol{w}\big),\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

(4.19)

\displaystyle\quad+t\kappa_{2}\varepsilon\left\langle\Delta\big(((\boldsymbol{u}+\boldsymbol{v})\cdot\boldsymbol{w})\boldsymbol{v}\big),\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}-t\varepsilon\left\langle\Delta\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{u})-\Delta\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{v}),\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}.

Similarly, using (
4.4
), we have

\displaystyle-t\gamma\left\langle\boldsymbol{v}\times\boldsymbol{B},\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle=-t\kappa_{1}\gamma\left\langle\boldsymbol{v}\times\boldsymbol{w},\Delta\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+t\kappa_{2}\gamma\left\langle\boldsymbol{v}\times|\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}\boldsymbol{w},\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

(4.20)

\displaystyle\quad-t\gamma\left\langle\boldsymbol{v}\times\big(\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{u})-\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{v})\big),\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}.

Writing
\eta=\sigma-\kappa_{1}\varepsilon
, we add (
4.2
), (
4.2
), (
4.2
), and (
4.2
) to obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left(t\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\right)+t\varepsilon\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+t\eta\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{2}+t\kappa_{1}\sigma\right)\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}-t\kappa_{2}\sigma\left\langle\Delta(|\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}\boldsymbol{w}),\Delta\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+t\kappa_{2}\sigma\left\langle\Delta\big(((\boldsymbol{u}+\boldsymbol{v})\cdot\boldsymbol{w})\boldsymbol{v}\big),\Delta\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+t\sigma\left\langle\Delta\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{u})-\Delta\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{v}),\Delta\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+t\sigma\left\langle\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{w}),\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+t\kappa_{2}\varepsilon\left\langle\Delta\big(|\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}\boldsymbol{w}\big),\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+t\kappa_{2}\varepsilon\left\langle\Delta\big(((\boldsymbol{u}+\boldsymbol{v})\cdot\boldsymbol{w})\boldsymbol{v}\big),\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}-t\varepsilon\left\langle\Delta\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{u})-\Delta\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{v}),\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}-t\varepsilon\left\langle\Delta\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{w}),\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad-t\gamma\left\langle\boldsymbol{w}\times\boldsymbol{H}_{1},\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}-t\kappa_{1}\gamma\left\langle\boldsymbol{v}\times\boldsymbol{w},\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+t\kappa_{2}\gamma\left\langle\boldsymbol{v}\times|\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}\boldsymbol{w},\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad-t\gamma\left\langle\boldsymbol{v}\times\big(\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{u})-\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{v})\big),\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}-t\gamma\left\langle\boldsymbol{w}\times\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{u}),\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}-t\gamma\left\langle\boldsymbol{v}\times\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{w}),\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+t\left\langle\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{u})-\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{v}),\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+t\left\langle\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{u})-\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{v}),\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{w}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

(4.21)

\displaystyle=J_{1}+J_{2}+\cdots+J_{17}.

It remains to estimate each of the terms in the last line. The first term is left as is. For the second term, integrating by parts, then applying Hölder’s and Young’s inequalities, we obtain

\displaystyle\left|{J_{2}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq t\kappa_{2}\sigma\left\|{\nabla(|\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}\boldsymbol{w})}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq Ct\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}^{2}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}^{2}+Ct\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}^{4}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}^{2}+\frac{t\eta}{16}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq Ct\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+Ct\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{4}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{t\eta}{16}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

where in the last step we used the Sobolev embedding
\mathbb{H}^{1}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{L}^{6}
. Similarly, for the third term we have

\displaystyle\left|{J_{3}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq Ct\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}\right)\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+Ct\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{4}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{4}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{t\eta}{16}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

For the term
J_{4}
, we integrate by parts, then apply (
3
) with
p=q=6
and Young’s inequality to obtain

\displaystyle\left|{J_{4}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq Ct\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{4}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{4}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+Ct\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}\right)\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+\frac{t\eta}{16}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

For the next term, integrating by parts once and applying (
2.48
) we have

\displaystyle\left|{J_{5}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq Ct\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+\frac{t\eta}{16}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

For the term
J_{6}
, by (
2.14
), Hölder’s and Young’s inequalities, we have

\displaystyle\left|{J_{6}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq Ct\varepsilon\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}^{4}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}^{2}+Ct\varepsilon\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}^{2}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}^{2}+Ct\varepsilon\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{2}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+Ct\varepsilon\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}^{4}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}^{2}+\frac{t\varepsilon}{16}\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq Ct\varepsilon\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+Ct\varepsilon\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{4}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{t\varepsilon}{16}\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

where in the last step we used the Gagliardo–Nirenberg inequalities.
Similarly, for the term
J_{7}
we have

\displaystyle\left|{J_{7}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq Ct\varepsilon\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}\right)\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}^{2}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}^{2}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+Ct\varepsilon\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{4}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{4}\right)\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{t\varepsilon}{16}\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

For the term
J_{8}
, by Young’s inequality and (
4
),

\displaystyle\left|{J_{8}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq Ct\varepsilon\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+Ct\varepsilon\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{4}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{4}\right)\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+Ct\varepsilon\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}\right)\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}^{2}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}^{2}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+\frac{t\varepsilon}{16}\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

For the next term, by (
2.48
) and Young’s inequality, we have

\displaystyle\left|{J_{9}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq Ct\varepsilon\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{t\varepsilon}{16}\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

For the term
J_{10}
, by Young’s inequality, Sobolev embedding, and the definition of
\boldsymbol{H}_{1}
,

\displaystyle\left|{J_{10}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq Ct\varepsilon^{-1}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{H}_{1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{3}}^{2}+\frac{t\varepsilon}{16}\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq Ct\varepsilon^{-1}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}^{2}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}\right)+\frac{t\varepsilon}{16}\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

Similarly, for the next two terms, applying Young’s inequality and Sobolev embedding, we have

\displaystyle\left|{J_{11}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq Ct\varepsilon^{-1}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{2}+\frac{t\varepsilon}{16}\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq Ct\varepsilon^{-1}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+\frac{t\varepsilon}{16}\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

\displaystyle\left|{J_{12}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq Ct\varepsilon^{-1}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}^{4}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}^{2}+\frac{t\varepsilon}{16}\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq Ct\varepsilon^{-1}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{4}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+\frac{t\varepsilon}{16}\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

For the term
J_{13}
, using Young’s inequality and (
2.20
) with
p=q=6
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left|{J_{13}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq Ct\varepsilon^{-1}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{2}\left\|{\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{u})-\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{v})}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{t\varepsilon}{16}\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq Ct\varepsilon^{-1}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{2}\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{4}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{4}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+\frac{t\varepsilon}{16}\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

For the terms
J_{14}
and
J_{15}
, we integrate by parts and use Young’s inequality and (
2.48
) to obtain

\displaystyle\left|{J_{14}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq Ct\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}+Ct\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+\frac{t\eta}{16}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

\displaystyle\left|{J_{15}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq Ct\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}+Ct\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+\frac{t\eta}{16}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

Finally, for the last two terms, by (
2.32
) and (
2.44
) we have

\displaystyle\left|{J_{16}}\right|+\left|{J_{17}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq Ct\varepsilon^{-1}\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+\frac{t\varepsilon}{16}\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

Altogether, substituting these estimates into (
4.2
) (and applying Agmon’s inequality), we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left(t\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\right)+t\varepsilon\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+t\eta\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq Ct\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{4}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{4}\right)\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}^{2}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}^{2}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+C\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}+Ct\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{4}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{4}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}^{2}.

Integrating both sides over
(0,t)
, then using Proposition
3.10
, Theorem
3.11
, and Lemma
4.8
, we have

\displaystyle t\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{w}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq C(1+t)e^{Ct^{3}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}-\boldsymbol{v}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2},

where
C
depends only on the coefficients of the equation,
\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}
and
\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}
,
as required.
∎

4.3.
Existence of global attractor

In light of Theorem
3.11
, for
k=1
or
2
, the system (
1.1
) generates a strongly continuous semigroup

(4.22)

\boldsymbol{S}(t):\mathbb{H}^{k}\to\mathbb{H}^{k},\quad\boldsymbol{S}(t)\boldsymbol{u}_{0}=\boldsymbol{u}(t)\quad\text{for }t\geq 0,

and thus
\big(\mathbb{H}^{k},\{\boldsymbol{S}(t)\}_{t\geq 0}\big)
is a semi-dynamical system.

The following theorem on the existence of global attractor for (
4.22
) is immediate.

Theorem 4.10
.

For
k=1
or
2
, the semi-dynamical system
\big(\mathbb{H}^{k},\{\boldsymbol{S}(t)\}_{t\geq 0}\big)
generated by (
1.1
) has a connected global attractor
\mathcal{A}
in the sense of Definition
4.1
.

Proof.

For
k=1
or
2
, the existence of a compact absorbing set is furnished by (
3.71
). Noting that the embedding
\mathbb{H}^{k+1}(\mathscr{O})\subset\mathbb{H}^{k}(\mathscr{O})
is compact (for a regular bounded domain
\mathscr{O}
) and applying Theorem
4.2
, we have the result.
∎

We now consider a special case of (
1.1
) where the spin current is not present and the only contribution to the effective magnetic field is the exchange field and the , i.e.
\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{u})
,
\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{u})
, and
\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{u})
are all set to zero. In this case, we obtain that the set of fixed points of
S(t)
defined by (
4.22
) is

(4.23)

\mathcal{N}=\big\{\boldsymbol{u}\in\text{D}(\Delta):\Delta\boldsymbol{u}+\kappa_{1}\boldsymbol{u}-\kappa_{2}|\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}\boldsymbol{u}=\boldsymbol{0}\big\}.

This can be seen by formally setting
\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}=\boldsymbol{0}
, taking dot product of the first equation in (
1.1
) with
\boldsymbol{H}
, and integrating by parts. The set of fixed points (
4.23
) corresponds to solutions of the stationary vector-valued Allen–Cahn equation, the structure of which is still an area of active research
[
1
,
50
]
. Next, we show that
\boldsymbol{S}(t)
admits a global Lyapunov function.

Proposition 4.11
.

The continuous function
\mathcal{L}:\mathbb{H}^{1}\to\mathbb{R}
defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\big(\boldsymbol{u}(t)\big):=\frac{1}{2}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\kappa_{2}}{4}\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}(t)|^{2}-\kappa_{1}/\kappa_{2}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

is a global Lyapunov function for
\boldsymbol{S}(t)
in the sense of Definition
4.3
.

Proof.

Applying the same argument leading to (
3
) yields the inequality

(4.24)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\mathcal{L}(\boldsymbol{u}(t))=-\left\langle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}(t),\boldsymbol{H}(t)\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}=-\sigma\left\|{\boldsymbol{H}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}-\varepsilon\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{H}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq 0,

which shows that the function
t\mapsto\mathcal{L}(\boldsymbol{S}(t)\boldsymbol{u}_{0})
is non-increasing. Furthermore, if
\mathcal{L}(\boldsymbol{S}(T)\boldsymbol{u}_{0})=\mathcal{L}(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})
, then integrating (
4.24
) over
(0,T)
gives
\boldsymbol{H}(t)=0
for all
t\in[0,T]
. This implies
\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{u}=0
, i.e.
\boldsymbol{u}(t)=\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
for all
t\in[0,T]
, which shows
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
is a fixed point. This proves
\mathcal{L}
is a global Lyapunov function for
\boldsymbol{S}(t)
.
∎

Theorem 4.12
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be the global attractor for
\boldsymbol{S}(t)
and
\mathcal{F}
be its set of fixed points (
4.23
). Then
\omega(\boldsymbol{u}_{0})\subset\mathcal{F}
for every
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in\mathbb{H}^{r}
. Moreover,
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{M}^{\rm{un}}(\mathcal{F})
.

Proof.

This follows from Theorem
4.10
and Proposition
4.4
.
∎

Furthermore, we will show that in fact
\boldsymbol{u}(t)
converges to some function
\varphi\in\omega(\boldsymbol{u})\subset\mathcal{F}
as
t\to+\infty
with some upper estimates on the rate. To this end, we will use some results on the Łojasiewicz–Simon gradient inequality applied to a gradient-like system
[
4
,
10
,
11
]
.

Theorem 4.13
.

There exists
\boldsymbol{\varphi}\in\omega(\boldsymbol{u})
such that

\lim_{t\to+\infty}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}(t)-\boldsymbol{\varphi}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}=0.

Moreover, as
t\to+\infty
,

\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}(t)-\boldsymbol{\varphi}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{-1}}=\begin{cases}O(e^{-ct})\quad&\text{if $\theta=\frac{1}{2}$},\\[2.84526pt]
O\big(t^{-\theta/(1-2\theta)}\big)\quad&\text{if $\theta=(0,\frac{1}{2})$},\end{cases}

where
\theta
is the Łojasiewicz exponent of
\mathcal{E}
and
c
is a constant.

Proof.

We will apply
[
11
, Theorem 1 and 2]
. The global Lyapunov function
\mathcal{L}
satisfies the Łojasiewicz–Simon gradient inequality, as shown in
[
10
]
and
[
54
, Section 3.6]
, so it remains to verify the so-called angle and comparability conditions
[
4
, conditions (AC+C)]
. In our context, we will show that there is a constant
C>0
such that

(4.25)

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathcal{L}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{u}),-\sigma\boldsymbol{H}+\varepsilon\Delta\boldsymbol{H}+\gamma\boldsymbol{u}\times\boldsymbol{H}\right\rangle_{(\mathbb{H}^{1})^{*},\mathbb{H}^{1}}\geq C\left(\left\|{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{u})}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{-1}}^{2}+\left\|{-\sigma\boldsymbol{H}+\varepsilon\Delta\boldsymbol{H}+\gamma\boldsymbol{u}\times\boldsymbol{H}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{-1}}^{2}\right)

for all
\boldsymbol{u}
in a neighbourhood of
\boldsymbol{\varphi}\in\omega(\boldsymbol{u})
, i.e.
\boldsymbol{u}\in N_{R}(\boldsymbol{\varphi}):=\{\boldsymbol{z}\in\omega(\boldsymbol{u}):\left\|{\boldsymbol{z}-\boldsymbol{\varphi}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}\leq R\}
. We have
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{u})=-\boldsymbol{H}
in
\mathbb{H}^{-1}
. Therefore, noting that

(4.26)

\left\langle\mathcal{L}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{u}),-\sigma\boldsymbol{H}+\varepsilon\Delta\boldsymbol{H}+\gamma\boldsymbol{u}\times\boldsymbol{H}\right\rangle_{(\mathbb{H}^{1})^{*},\mathbb{H}^{1}}=\sigma\left\|{\boldsymbol{H}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{H}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{u})}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{-1}}^{2}+\left\|{-\sigma\boldsymbol{H}+\varepsilon\Delta\boldsymbol{H}+\gamma\boldsymbol{u}\times\boldsymbol{H}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{-1}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{\boldsymbol{H}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{-1}}^{2}+\sigma\left\|{\boldsymbol{H}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{-1}}^{2}+\varepsilon\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{H}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{-1}}^{2}+\gamma\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}\times\boldsymbol{H}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{-1}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{H}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon\left\|{\boldsymbol{H}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+C\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{3}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{H}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\sigma\left\|{\boldsymbol{H}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{H}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\right),

where we used the embedding
\mathbb{L}^{6/5}\subset\mathbb{H}^{-1}
, Hölder’s inequality, the fact that
\boldsymbol{u}\in N_{R}(\boldsymbol{\varphi})
, and (
3.23
). This proves (
4.25
), thus completing the proof of the theorem.
∎

4.4.
Existence of exponential attractor

It is known that, while the global attractor has many desirable properties as an appropriate object to study when considering long-time behaviour, it may attract trajectories at a very slow rate and is sensitive to perturbation. Furthermore, it is in general very difficult to express the convergence rate only in terms of the physical parameters of the problem. As such, it is argued in
[
17
]
that one should consider a larger object which are more robust under perturbation, attract trajectories at a fast rate, but are still finite dimensional. Such an object is called an exponential attractor, whose construction is explained in
[
40
]
.

Definition 4.14

(Exponential attractor)
.

A subset
\mathcal{M}\subseteq X
is an
exponential attractor
for
S(t)
if

(1)

it is compact in
X
,

(2)

it has finite fractal dimension,
\text{dim}_{\mathrm{F}}\mathcal{M}<+\infty
,

(3)

it is semi-invariant, i.e.
S(t)\mathcal{M}\subseteq\mathcal{M}
,
\forall t\geq 0
,

(4)

it attracts exponentially fast bounded subsets of
X
in the following sense: for all bounded set
B\subset X
, there exists a constant
c
depending on
B
and
\alpha\geq 0
such that

\text{dist}\big(S(t)B,\mathcal{M}\big)\leq ce^{-\alpha t},\quad\forall t\geq 0.

Therefore, an exponential attractor, if it exists, contains the global attractor and implies the finite dimensionality of the global attractor. The existence of an exponential attractor has been shown for various models of physical significance.
To show the existence of an exponential attractor, we follow the general ideas from
[
40
]
(also see
[
41
]
).

Theorem 4.15

(
[
40
]
)
.

Let
X
and
H
be two Hilbert spaces such that
X\hookrightarrow H
is a compact embedding, and let
S(t):E\to E
be a strongly continuous semigroup acting on a subset
E\subseteq X
. For a fixed
R>0
, let

B_{R}:=\{x\in E:\left\|{x}\right\|_{H}\leq R\}.

Suppose that

(1)

the smoothing property holds, i.e. for all
x_{1},x_{2}\in B_{R}
, and
t>0
,

\left\|{S(t)x_{1}-S(t)x_{2}}\right\|_{X}\leq h(t)\left\|{x_{1}-x_{2}}\right\|_{H},

where
h
is a continuous function of
t
, which may depend on
R
.

(2)

for any
T>0
and
x\in B_{R}
, the map
t\mapsto S(t)x
is Hölder continuous on
[0,T]
,

(3)

for any
t\in[0,T]
, the map
x\mapsto S(t)x
is Lipschitz continuous on
B_{R}
.

Then
S(t)
possesses an exponential attractor
\mathcal{M}
on
H
.

Theorem 4.16
.

The semi-dynamical system generated by (
1.1
) has an exponential attractor
\mathcal{M}
on
\mathbb{H}^{1}
in the sense of Definition
4.14
.

Proof.

It remains to verify the conditions in Theorem
4.15
, where
X=\mathbb{H}^{2}
and
E=H=\mathbb{H}^{1}
. Smoothing property is inferred from Lemma
4.9
. Lipschitz continuity on
B_{R}
is given by Lemma
4.8
. Hölder continuity in time can be shown as in
[
48
, Theorem 2.3]
. This completes the proof of the theorem.
∎

5.
Further properties of the attractor

In this section, we will study further properties of the attractor, such as its fractal dimension as well as the existence of a family of exponential attractors for (
1.1
) which are continuous with respect to the parameter
\varepsilon
. The latter also implies the existence of an exponential attractor, hence also global attractor with finite fractal dimension, for the Landau–Lifshitz–Bloch equation with spin torque terms. Throughout this section, we will write
\boldsymbol{S}_{\varepsilon}(t)
for the semigroup generated by the system (
1.1
) to highlight the dependence on
\varepsilon
.

5.1.
Fractal dimension of the global attractor

We aim find an upper bound for the fractal dimension of the global attractor. First, we need the following lemma.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
\boldsymbol{S}_{\varepsilon}(t)
be the semigroup generated by (
1.1
) with global attractor
\mathcal{A}
. Let
\boldsymbol{u}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\in\mathcal{A}
, and write
\boldsymbol{u}(t):=\boldsymbol{S}_{\varepsilon}(t)\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
and
\boldsymbol{v}(t):=\boldsymbol{S}_{\varepsilon}(t)\boldsymbol{v}_{0}
. For any
t>0
,

(5.1)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}(t)-\boldsymbol{v}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}(s)-\Delta\boldsymbol{v}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}(s)-\nabla\boldsymbol{v}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s\leq Ce^{C\varepsilon^{-5}t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}-\boldsymbol{v}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

Moreover,

(5.2)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}(t)-\boldsymbol{v}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}\leq Ct^{-1}\left(1+t\varepsilon^{-8}\right)e^{C\varepsilon^{-5}t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}-\boldsymbol{v}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

where
C
is independent of
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
,
\varepsilon
, and
t
.

Proof.

Let
\boldsymbol{w}(t)=\boldsymbol{u}(t)-\boldsymbol{v}(t)
. We have, as in (
4.10
),

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(1+\varepsilon^{-1}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{2}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{4}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{4}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(1+\varepsilon^{-5}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

where in the last step we used the Gagliardo–Nirenberg inequalities, (
3.23
), and (
3.42
), noting that
\boldsymbol{u}(t),\boldsymbol{v}(t)\in\mathcal{A}
. An application of the Gronwall inequality gives (
5.1
).

By similar argument as in the proof of Lemma
4.8
, but instead successively taking the inner product with
-t\Delta\boldsymbol{w}
in (
4.2
), with
-\sigma t\Delta\boldsymbol{w}
in (
4.2
), and with
\varepsilon t\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{w}
in (
4.2
), we obtain an inequality analogous to (
4.16
):

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left(t\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}\right)+\varepsilon t\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+t\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left(t\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\right)+\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+Ct\,\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+Ct\varepsilon\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2},

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})

\displaystyle:=1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{4}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{4}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}^{2}+\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{4}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{4}\right)\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}\right).

Integrating over
(0,t)
, noting (
3.67
) and using (
5.1
), we obtain (
5.2
).
∎

The following theorem shows that the fractal dimension of the global attractor is finite.

Theorem 5.2
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be the global attractor of the semi-dynamical system generated by (
1.1
). Then

\text{dim}_{\mathrm{F}}\mathcal{A}\leq C\varepsilon^{-4d},

where
C
may depend on the coefficients of (
1.1
), but is independent of
\varepsilon
.
In particular,
\mathcal{A}
has a finite fractal dimension.

Proof.

We take
t=\varepsilon^{8}
in (
5.2
) to obtain

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{S}_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{8})\boldsymbol{u}_{0}-\boldsymbol{S}_{\varepsilon}(\varepsilon^{8})\boldsymbol{v}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}\leq C\varepsilon^{-4}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}-\boldsymbol{v}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}},\quad\forall\boldsymbol{u}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\in\mathcal{A}

where
C
is a constant independent of
\varepsilon
(but may depend on other parameters of (
1.1
)). Theorem
4.6
and Theorem
A.3
then imply the required result.
∎

5.2.
A family of robust exponential attractors

It is known that in general, the global attractor is sensitive to a perturbation of parameter. There exist abstract conditions that guarantee continuous dependence of the global attractor on a parameter, however it is difficult to verify in practice
[
57
]
.

Here, we show the robustness (continuity) of the exponential attractor with respect to the parameter
\varepsilon
for the case
d\leq 2
and
\lambda_{2}=0
, i.e. the higher-order term of the anisotropy field
\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}
is assumed to be negligible (which is physically reasonable). To this end, we need to obtain estimates for the solution and the difference of two solutions which are
uniform in
\varepsilon
.
First, we derive uniform estimates analogous to Proposition
3.1
, Proposition
3.2
, and Proposition
3.3
in the following lemma. We write
\eta:=\sigma-\kappa_{1}\varepsilon>0
as before.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
\boldsymbol{u}
be the solution of (
1.1
) with initial data
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in\mathbb{H}^{1}
, whose corresponding effective field is
\boldsymbol{H}
. The following statements hold:

(1)

For all
t\geq 0
,

(5.3)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

(5.4)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{Ct}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2},

where
C
is independent of
\varepsilon
,
t
, and
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
.

(2)

For all
t\geq 0
,

(5.5)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{4}\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}(s)||\nabla\boldsymbol{u}(s)|}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s\leq C(1+t)\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

(5.6)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{H}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}(s)||\nabla\boldsymbol{u}(s)|}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s\leq Ce^{Ct}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2},

where
C
is independent of
\varepsilon
,
t
, and
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
.

(3)

There exists
t_{1}
depending on
\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}
(but is independent of
\varepsilon
) such that for all
t\geq t_{1}
,

(5.7)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+\int_{t}^{t+1}\left(\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}(s)||\nabla\boldsymbol{u}(s)|}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}s\leq\rho_{1},

where
\rho_{1}
is independent of
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
,
\varepsilon
, and
t
.

(4)

Let
\delta>0
be arbitrary. For all
t\geq\delta
,

(5.8)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+\int_{t}^{t+\delta}\left(\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}(s)||\nabla\boldsymbol{u}(s)|}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}s\leq\mu_{1},

where
\mu_{1}
depends on
\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}
, but is independent of
\varepsilon
and
t
.

(5)

For all
t>0
,

(5.9)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}\leq M_{0}\left(1+t+t^{-1}\right),

where
M_{0}
is independent of
\varepsilon
and
t
, but may depend on other coefficients of (
1.1
),
|\mathscr{O}|
, and
\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}
.

Proof.

The proof of (
5.3
) and (
5.5
) is established in Proposition
3.1
, noting that the estimates in Proposition
3.1
is already uniform in
\varepsilon
. Next, we show (
5.4
) for
k=1
.
Taking the inner product of both equations in (
1.1
) with
-\Delta\boldsymbol{u}
and rearranging the terms, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\eta\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+2\kappa_{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\kappa_{2}\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}||\nabla\boldsymbol{u}|}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=-\sigma\left\langle\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{u}),\Delta\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}-\varepsilon\kappa_{2}\left\langle\nabla(|\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}\boldsymbol{u}),\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\varepsilon\left\langle\Delta\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{u}),\Delta\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+\gamma\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}\times\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{u}),\Delta\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\gamma\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}\times\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{u}),\Delta\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}-\left\langle\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{u}),\Delta\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}-\left\langle\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{u}),\Delta\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

(5.10)

\displaystyle=:I_{1}+I_{2}+\cdots+I_{7}.

We will estimate each term on the last line. For the first and the third terms, by Young’s inequality and (
2.48
), we have

\displaystyle\left|{I_{1}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\eta}{6}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

\displaystyle\left|{I_{3}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\eta}{6}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

For the second term, by (
2.12
), Young’s and Agmon’s inequalities (for
d\leq 2
), we have

\displaystyle\left|{I_{2}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{4}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq C\varepsilon\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

For the term
I_{4}
and
I_{5}
(noting that we assumed
\lambda_{2}=0
), similarly we have

\displaystyle\left|{I_{4}}\right|+\left|{I_{5}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{4}+\frac{\eta}{6}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

Finally, for the terms
I_{6}
and
I_{7}
, we apply (
2.37
) and (
2.41
) respectively, to obtain

\displaystyle\left|{I_{6}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{4}\right)\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\eta}{6}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

\displaystyle\left|{I_{7}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{4}\right)+\frac{\eta}{6}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

Substituting these estimates into (
5.2
), we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

(5.11)

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+C\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{4}+C\varepsilon^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}+C\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{4}+\varepsilon\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}\right)\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

Integrating this over
(0,t)
, and applying the Gronwall inequality (noting Proposition
3.1
), we obtain (
5.4
). The estimate (
5.6
) then follows by noting the definition of
H
and applying Hölder’s inequality. Inequalities (
5.7
) and (
5.8
) also follow from (
5.2
) and the uniform Gronwall inequality.

Finally, by the same argument as in Proposition
3.2
, we infer inequality (
5.9
) from the uniform Gronwall inequality and (
5.7
). This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Furthermore, we need another estimate for
\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}
which is uniform in
\varepsilon
.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
\boldsymbol{u}
be the solution of (
1.1
) with initial data
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
, whose corresponding effective field is
\boldsymbol{H}
. The following statements hold:

(1)

For all
t\geq 0
,

(5.12)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s\leq\kappa_{2}(t),

where
\kappa_{2}(t)
is an increasing function of
t
which also depends on
\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}
, but is independent of
\varepsilon
.

(2)

There exists
t_{2}
depending on
\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}
(but is independent of
\varepsilon
) such that for all
t\geq t_{2}
,

(5.13)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}+\int_{t}^{t+1}\left(\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}s\leq\rho_{2},

where
\rho_{2}
is independent of
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
,
\varepsilon
, and
t
.

(3)

For all
t>0
,

(5.14)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}\leq C\left(1+t^{-1}\right)\exp(e^{Ct}),

where
C
is independent of
\varepsilon
and
t
, but may depend on other coefficients of (
1.1
),
|\mathscr{O}|
, and
\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}
.

Proof.

Taking the inner product of both equations in (
1.1
) with
\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}
and integrating by parts whenever appropriate, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\eta\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\kappa_{1}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\kappa_{2}\left\langle\nabla\big(|\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}\boldsymbol{u}\big),\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}-\sigma\left\langle\nabla\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{u}),\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+\kappa_{2}\varepsilon\left\langle\Delta\big(|\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}\boldsymbol{u}\big),\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}-\varepsilon\left\langle\Delta\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{u}),\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}-\varepsilon\left\langle\Delta\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{u}),\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+\gamma\left\langle\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\times\Delta\boldsymbol{u},\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\gamma\left\langle\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\times\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{u}),\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\gamma\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}\times\nabla\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{u}),\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+\gamma\left\langle\nabla\boldsymbol{u}\times\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{u}),\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\gamma\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}\times\nabla\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{u}),\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}-\left\langle\nabla\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{u}),\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}-\left\langle\nabla\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{u}),\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

(5.15)

\displaystyle=I_{1}+I_{2}+\cdots+I_{13}.

We will estimate each term on the last line. The first term is left as is. For the terms
I_{2}
and
I_{3}
, by Young’s inequality and Sobolev embedding,

\displaystyle\left|{I_{2}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq 3\kappa_{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}^{2}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{6}}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{4}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\eta}{12}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

\displaystyle\left|{I_{3}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sigma\left\|{\nabla\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{u})}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+\frac{\eta}{12}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

For the next term, by (
2.14
), Hölder’s and Young’s inequalities, and Agmon’s inequality in 2D, we have

\displaystyle\left|{I_{4}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon\left\|{\Delta\big(|\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}\boldsymbol{u}\big)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon}{8}\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{2}+C\varepsilon\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{4}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon}{8}\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{3}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}+C\varepsilon\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon}{8}\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{4}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon}{8}\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

By similar argument, we also have

\displaystyle\left|{I_{5}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{4}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon}{8}\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

and

\displaystyle\left|{I_{6}}\right|\leq C\varepsilon\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon}{8}\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

For the next three terms, by the Young and the Gagliardo–Nirenberg inequalities, and the Sobolev embedding, we have

\displaystyle\left|{I_{7}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\gamma\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{1/2}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{3/2}\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{4}+\frac{\eta}{12}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

\displaystyle\left|{I_{8}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\gamma\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}\left\|{\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{u})}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{2}\right)\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\eta}{12}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq C\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}\right)\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\eta}{12}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

\displaystyle\left|{I_{9}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\gamma\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}\left\|{\nabla\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{u})}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{2}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}^{2}+\frac{\eta}{12}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq C\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}\right)\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\eta}{12}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

For the terms
I_{10}
and
I_{11}
, using (
2.48
), similarly we obtain

\displaystyle\left|{I_{10}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\gamma\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}\left\|{\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{u})}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\eta}{12}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

\displaystyle\left|{I_{11}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\gamma\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}\left\|{\nabla\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{u})}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\eta}{12}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

Finally, for the last two terms we apply (
2.38
) and (
2.42
) to obtain

\displaystyle\left|{I_{12}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\nu_{\infty}\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{4}+\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{4}\right)+\frac{\eta}{12}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

\displaystyle\left|{I_{13}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}||\nabla\boldsymbol{u}|}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\right)+\frac{\eta}{12}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

Altogether, we obtain from (
5.2
),

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{4}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}+C\varepsilon\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{4}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}^{2}

(5.16)

\displaystyle\quad+C\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{4}+\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}||\nabla\boldsymbol{u}|}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{4}\right).

Integrating over
(0,t)
and applying Lemma
5.3
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}+Ce^{Ct}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{6}+C\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{4}\mathrm{d}s.

The inequality (
5.12
) then follows from the Gronwall inequality.

If we take the inner product of (
1.1
) with
t\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}
and follow the same argument as before, instead of (
5.2
) we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left(t\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\right)+t\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+t\varepsilon\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+Ct\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{4}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}+Ct\varepsilon\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{4}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+Ct\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{4}+\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}||\nabla\boldsymbol{u}|}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{4}\right),

which upon integration over
(0,t)
yields

\displaystyle t\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq Ce^{Ct}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+Cte^{Ct}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{6}+C\int_{0}^{t}s\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{4}\mathrm{d}s.

Thus, (
5.14
) follows from the Gronwall inequality (noting Lemma
5.3
).

Next, from (
5.2
) and (
5.7
) we obtain for
t\geq t_{1}
,

(5.17)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(1+\rho_{1}^{3}\right)\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+C\varepsilon\rho_{1}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}^{2}+C\left(1+\rho_{1}^{2}+\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{4}\right).

Note that by (
5.7
), we have

\displaystyle\int_{t}^{t+1}C\left(1+\rho_{1}^{3}\right)\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s+\int_{t}^{t+1}C\varepsilon\rho_{1}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq C\left(1+\rho_{1}^{4}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\int_{t}^{t+1}C\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s\leq C\rho_{1},

where
C
is independent of
\varepsilon
. Therefore, by the uniform Gronwall inequality,

(5.18)

\displaystyle\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{u}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq C\left(1+\rho_{1}^{4}\right)\exp\left(\rho_{1}^{2}\right),\quad\forall t\geq t_{1}+1.

Finally, integrating (
5.17
) over
(t,t+1)
, then applying (
5.7
) and (
5.18
) yield (
5.13
).
This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
\boldsymbol{u}
be the solution of (
1.1
) with initial data
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
, whose corresponding effective field is
\boldsymbol{H}
. The following statements hold:

(1)

For all
t\geq 0
,

(5.19)

\displaystyle\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\nabla\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s\leq\kappa_{3}(t),

where
\kappa_{3}(t)
is an increasing function of
t
which also depends on
\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}
, but is independent of
\varepsilon
.

(2)

There exists
t_{3}
depending on
\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}
(but is independent of
\varepsilon
) such that for all
t\geq t_{3}
,

(5.20)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}^{2}+\int_{t}^{t+1}\left(\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon\left\|{\nabla\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}s\leq\rho_{3},

where
\rho_{3}
is independent of
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
,
\varepsilon
, and
t
.

Proof.

We apply the operator
-\Delta
, then take the inner product of both equations in (
1.1
) with
\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}
, and integrate by parts as necessary (whenever allowed) to obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\eta\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon\left\|{\nabla\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\kappa_{2}\sigma\left\langle\Delta(|\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}\boldsymbol{u}),\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}-\sigma\left\langle\Delta\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{u}),\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}-\sigma\left\langle\Delta\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{u}),\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad-\kappa_{2}\varepsilon\left\langle\nabla\Delta(|\boldsymbol{u}|^{2}\boldsymbol{u}),\nabla\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}-\kappa_{2}\varepsilon\left\langle\nabla\Delta\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{u}),\nabla\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\varepsilon\left\langle\Delta^{2}\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{u}),\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+\gamma\left\langle\Delta\big(\boldsymbol{u}\times\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{u})\big),\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\gamma\left\langle\Delta\big(\boldsymbol{u}\times\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{u})\big),\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}-\left\langle\Delta\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{u}),\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}-\left\langle\Delta\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{u}),\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

(5.21)

\displaystyle=I_{1}+I_{2}+\cdots+I_{10}.

By using Young’s inequality, (
2.12
), (
2.14
), (
2.17
), (
2.23
), (
2.39
), (
2.43
), and (
2.48
), without elaborating further, we obtain the following inequalities:

\displaystyle\left|{I_{1}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{6}+\frac{\eta}{9}\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

\displaystyle\left|{I_{2}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\eta}{9}\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

\displaystyle\left|{I_{3}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\eta}{9}\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

\displaystyle\left|{I_{4}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{4}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\left\|{\nabla\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\left|{I_{5}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{4}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\left\|{\nabla\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\left|{I_{6}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{4}}^{2}+\frac{\eta}{9}\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

\displaystyle\left|{I_{7}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{4}\right)+\frac{\eta}{9}\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

\displaystyle\left|{I_{8}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{4}\right)+\frac{\eta}{9}\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

\displaystyle\left|{I_{9}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\nu_{\infty}\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{4}\right)+C\nu_{\infty}\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}\right)\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\eta}{9}\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

\displaystyle\left|{I_{10}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{4}+\frac{\eta}{9}\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

Altogether, substituting these into (
5.2
) we have

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon\left\|{\nabla\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq C\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{4}\right)+C\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}\right)\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+C\varepsilon\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{4}}^{2}.

Integrating this over
(0,t)
and applying (
5.12
) yields (
5.19
). Applying the same argument as in the proof of (
5.13
) then gives (
5.20
). This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
\boldsymbol{u}
be the solution of (
1.1
) with initial data
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
, whose corresponding effective field is
\boldsymbol{H}
. The following statements hold:

(1)

For all
t\geq 0
,

(5.22)

\displaystyle\left\|{\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\nabla\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\Delta^{3}\boldsymbol{u}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s\leq\kappa_{4}(t),

where
\kappa_{4}(t)
is an increasing function of
t
which also depends on
\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{4}}
, but is independent of
\varepsilon
.

(2)

There exists
t_{4}
depending on
\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}
(but is independent of
\varepsilon
) such that for all
t\geq t_{4}
,

(5.23)

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{4}}^{2}+\int_{t}^{t+1}\left(\left\|{\nabla\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\varepsilon\left\|{\Delta^{3}\boldsymbol{u}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}s\leq\rho_{4},

where
\rho_{4}
is independent of
\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
,
\varepsilon
, and
t
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of Lemma
5.5
, but instead we apply the operator
\Delta^{2}
, then take the inner product of both equations in (
1.1
) with
\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}
.
∎

We will derive some estimates for the difference of two solutions which are uniform in
\varepsilon
. Recall that
\boldsymbol{S}_{\varepsilon}(t)
is the semigroup generated by the system (
1.1
). An analogue of Lemma
4.8
is stated below.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
\varepsilon\in[0,\sigma/\kappa_{1}]
, and let
\mathbb{B}\subset\mathbb{H}^{1}
be a semi-invariant absorbing set for
\boldsymbol{S}_{\varepsilon}(t)
furnished by Lemma
5.4
and Lemma
5.5
. There exists a constant
C
such that for all
t>0
,

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{S}_{\varepsilon}(t)\boldsymbol{u}_{0}-\boldsymbol{S}_{\varepsilon}(t)\boldsymbol{v}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}+\varepsilon\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{S}_{\varepsilon}(t)\boldsymbol{u}_{0}-\boldsymbol{S}_{\varepsilon}(t)\boldsymbol{v}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{S}_{\varepsilon}(t)\boldsymbol{u}_{0}-\boldsymbol{S}_{\varepsilon}(t)\boldsymbol{v}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\beta_{1}(t)\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}-\boldsymbol{v}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2},\quad\forall\boldsymbol{u}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\in\mathbb{B},

where
\beta_{1}(t)
is an increasing function of
t
which is independent of
\varepsilon
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of Lemma
4.8
, noting that we now have (
5.13
) for
\boldsymbol{u}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\in\mathbb{B}
. Further details are omitted.
∎

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
\varepsilon\in[0,\sigma/\kappa_{1}]
, and let
\mathbb{B}\subset\mathbb{H}^{1}
be a semi-invariant absorbing set for
\boldsymbol{S}_{\varepsilon}(t)
furnished by Lemma
5.4
and Lemma
5.5
. There exists a constant
C
such that for all
t>0
,

(5.24)

\left\|{\boldsymbol{S}_{\varepsilon}(t)\boldsymbol{u}_{0}-\boldsymbol{S}_{\varepsilon}(t)\boldsymbol{v}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}\leq C\left(1+t^{-1}\right)\beta_{1}(t)\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}_{0}-\boldsymbol{v}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}},\quad\forall\boldsymbol{u}_{0},\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\in\mathbb{B},

where
\beta_{1}(t)
is an increasing function of
t
in Lemma
5.7
(which is independent of
\varepsilon
).

Proof.

We repeat the steps of the proof of Lemma
4.9
with
\boldsymbol{w}(t):=\boldsymbol{S}_{\varepsilon}(t)\boldsymbol{u}_{0}-\boldsymbol{S}_{\varepsilon}(t)\boldsymbol{v}_{0}
to obtain (
4.2
). Each term
J_{i}
, where
i=1,2,\ldots,16
, is then estimated as before, except for the terms
J_{10}
,
J_{11}
,
J_{12}
,
J_{13}
,
J_{16}
, and
J_{17}
. The estimates for these terms in the proof of Lemma
4.9
still depend on
\varepsilon^{-1}
, thus they need to be estimated differently here.

For the term
J_{10}
, by the definition of
\boldsymbol{H}_{1}
and (
5.12
), after integrating by parts we have

\displaystyle\left|{J_{10}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq t\gamma\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{H}_{1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+t\gamma\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{H}_{1}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq Ct\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{4}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{t\eta}{16}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

Similarly, for the terms
J_{11}
and
J_{12}
, we have

\displaystyle\left|{J_{11}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq t\kappa_{1}\gamma\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+t\kappa_{1}\gamma\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq Ct\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{t\eta}{16}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

\displaystyle\left|{J_{12}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq t\kappa_{2}\gamma\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{8}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{8}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{8}}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+2t\kappa_{2}\gamma\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{8}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{8}}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{8}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{8}}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+t\kappa_{2}\gamma\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{8}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{8}}^{2}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{8}}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq Ct\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{4}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{t\eta}{16}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

For the term
J_{13}
, we integrate by parts and apply (
3
) with
p=q=6
to obtain

\displaystyle\left|{J_{13}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq t\gamma\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}\left\|{\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{u})-\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{v})}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{4}}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+t\gamma\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}\left\|{\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{u})-\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{v})}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq Ct\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{6}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{6}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+\frac{t\eta}{16}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

For the terms
J_{16}
and
J_{17}
, we infer from (
2.6
) and (
2.45
) that

\displaystyle\left|{J_{16}}\right|+\left|{J_{17}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq Ct\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{t\eta}{16}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{w}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}.

These estimates, together with estimates for the other terms derived in Lemma
4.9
and Lemma
5.7
, yield

\displaystyle t\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}(t)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{0}^{t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s+C\int_{0}^{t}s\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{4}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{4}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\quad+C\int_{0}^{t}s\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{4}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\quad+C\varepsilon\int_{0}^{t}s\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}\right)\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}^{2}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{3}}^{2}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}(s)}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq C\beta_{1}(t)\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+C\left(1+\rho_{2}^{2}\right)t\beta_{1}(t)\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+C\left(1+\rho_{1}^{2}\right)\rho_{3}t\beta_{1}(t)\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C(1+t)\beta_{1}(t)\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2},

where in the last step we used (
5.7
), (
5.13
), (
5.20
), and Lemma
5.7
. This implies the inequality (
5.24
).
∎

The following lemma shows a continuous dependence estimate on the parameter
\varepsilon
.

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
\varepsilon\in[0,\sigma/\kappa_{1}]
, and let
\mathbb{B}\subset\mathbb{H}^{1}
be a semi-invariant absorbing set for
\boldsymbol{S}_{\varepsilon}(t)
furnished by Lemma
5.4
and Lemma
5.5
. There exists a constant
C
such that for all
t\geq 0
,

\left\|{\boldsymbol{S}_{\varepsilon}(t)\boldsymbol{u}_{0}-\boldsymbol{S}_{0}(t)\boldsymbol{u}_{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}\leq C\varepsilon e^{Ct},\quad\forall\boldsymbol{u}_{0}\in\mathbb{B},

where
C
is independent of
\varepsilon
and
t
.

Proof.

Let
\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon}(t):=\boldsymbol{S}_{\varepsilon}(t)\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
and
\boldsymbol{u}^{0}(t):=\boldsymbol{S}_{0}(t)\boldsymbol{u}_{0}
. Let
\boldsymbol{v}(t):=\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon}(t)-\boldsymbol{u}^{0}(t)
. Then
\boldsymbol{v}
solves

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{v}

\displaystyle=\sigma\left(\Psi(\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon})-\Psi(\boldsymbol{u}^{0})\right)+\sigma\left(\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon})-\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{0})\right)+\sigma\left(\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon})-\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{0})\right)

\displaystyle\quad-\varepsilon\Delta\Psi(\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon})-\varepsilon\Delta\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon})-\varepsilon\Delta\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle\quad-\gamma\left(\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon}\times\big(\Psi(\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon})+\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon})+\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon})\big)-\boldsymbol{u}^{0}\times\big(\Psi(\boldsymbol{u}^{0})+\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{0})+\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{0})\big)\right)

\displaystyle\quad+\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon})-\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{u}^{0})+\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon})-\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{u}^{0})

\displaystyle=\eta\Delta\boldsymbol{v}+\sigma\kappa_{1}\boldsymbol{v}-\sigma\kappa_{2}|\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon}|^{2}\boldsymbol{v}-\sigma\kappa_{2}\left((\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon}+\boldsymbol{u}^{0})\cdot\boldsymbol{v}\right)\boldsymbol{u}^{0}+\sigma\left(\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon})-\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{0})\right)+\sigma\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{v})

\displaystyle\quad-\varepsilon\Delta^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon}-\varepsilon\kappa_{2}\Delta\big(|\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon}|^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon}\big)-\varepsilon\Delta\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon})-\varepsilon\Delta\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{v})-\gamma\boldsymbol{v}\times\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon}-\gamma\boldsymbol{u}^{0}\times\Delta\boldsymbol{v}-\gamma\boldsymbol{v}\times\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle\quad-\gamma\boldsymbol{u}^{0}\times\left(\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon})-\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{0})\right)-\gamma\boldsymbol{v}\times\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon})-\gamma\boldsymbol{u}^{0}\times\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{v})

(5.25)

\displaystyle\quad+\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon})-\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{u}^{0})+\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon})-\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{u}^{0}).

Taking the inner product of (
5.2
) with
\boldsymbol{v}
and integrating by parts as necessary, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\eta\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\sigma\kappa_{2}\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon}||\boldsymbol{v}|}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\sigma\kappa_{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}^{0}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sigma\kappa_{1}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}-\sigma\kappa_{2}\left\langle(\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon}\cdot\boldsymbol{v})\boldsymbol{u}^{0},\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\sigma\left\langle\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon})-\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{0}),\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\sigma\left\langle\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+\varepsilon\left\langle\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon},\nabla\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\varepsilon\kappa_{2}\left\langle\nabla\big(|\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon}|^{2}\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon}\big),\nabla\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\varepsilon\left\langle\nabla\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon}),\nabla\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}-\varepsilon\left\langle\Delta\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon}),\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad-\gamma\left\langle\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{0}\times\boldsymbol{v},\nabla\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}-\gamma\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}^{0}\times\left(\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon})-\Phi_{\mathrm{a}}(\boldsymbol{u}^{0})\right),\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}-\gamma\left\langle\boldsymbol{u}^{0}\times\Phi_{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{v}),\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

\displaystyle\quad+\left\langle\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon})-\mathcal{R}(\boldsymbol{u}^{0}),\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}+\left\langle\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon})-\mathcal{S}(\boldsymbol{u}^{0}),\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}

(5.26)

\displaystyle=:I_{1}+I_{2}+\cdots+I_{13}.

We estimate each term
I_{j}
, where
j=2,3,\ldots,13
, by using Hölder’s and Young’s inequalities in a standard way and noting that
\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon}\in\mathbb{B}
for
\varepsilon\in[0,\sigma/\kappa_{1}]
. Applying (
2.20
) and (
2.48
) as necessary, we obtain

\displaystyle\left|{I_{2}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sigma\kappa_{2}}{2}\left\|{|\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon}||\boldsymbol{v}|}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\sigma\kappa_{2}}{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}^{0}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

\displaystyle\left|{I_{3}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{4}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}^{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{4}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq C(1+\rho_{2}^{2})\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

\displaystyle\left|{I_{4}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\left|{I_{5}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon^{2}\left\|{\nabla\Delta\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\eta}{8}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq C\rho_{3}\varepsilon^{2}+\frac{\eta}{8}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

\displaystyle\left|{I_{6}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{4}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\eta}{8}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq C\rho_{2}^{3}\varepsilon^{2}+\frac{\eta}{8}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

\displaystyle\left|{I_{7}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{1}}^{2}+\frac{\eta}{8}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq C\rho_{1}\varepsilon^{2}+\frac{\eta}{8}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

\displaystyle\left|{I_{8}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon}}\right\|_{\mathbb{H}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\eta}{8}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq C\rho_{2}\varepsilon^{2}+\frac{\eta}{8}\left\|{\Delta\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

\displaystyle\left|{I_{9}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{u}^{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\eta}{8}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq C\rho_{3}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\eta}{8}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

\displaystyle\left|{I_{10}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}^{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{2}\right)\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}^{\varepsilon}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{4}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}^{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{4}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq C(1+\rho_{2}^{3})\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

\displaystyle\left|{I_{11}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}^{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq C\rho_{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

\displaystyle\left|{I_{12}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\left\|{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}(\mathscr{O};\mathbb{R}^{d})}\left(1+\left\|{\boldsymbol{u}^{0}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{\infty}}^{2}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\eta}{8}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}\leq C(1+\rho_{2})\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\eta}{8}\left\|{\nabla\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}}^{2},

\displaystyle\left|{I_{13}}\right|
